Modularity of higher theta series II: Chow group of the generic fiber
Tony Feng, Adeel A. Khan

Abstract
. Higher theta series on moduli spaces of Hermitian shtukas were constructed by Feng–Yun–Zhang and conjectured to be modular, parallel to classical conjectures in the Kudla program. In this paper we prove the modularity of higher theta series after restriction to the generic locus. The proof is an upgrade, using motivic homotopy theory, of earlier work of Feng–Yun–Zhang which established generic modularity of ℓ \ell -adic realizations. In the process, we develop some general tools of broader utility. One such is the motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence , a categorical trace formalism for extracting computations in the Chow group from computations in Voevodsky’s derived category of motives. Another new tool is the derived homogeneous Fourier transform , which we use to implement a form of Fourier analysis for motives.



1.
Introduction

In this paper we synthesize two threads of research in the theory of algebraic cycles. The first thread comes from the lineage of the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture, and broadly speaking concerns the relationship between algebraic cycles on arithmetic moduli spaces and special values of
L
-functions. The second thread comes from the lineage of the Milnor Conjecture, through the
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory introduced by Voevodsky, and concerns a cohomological approach to motives. As these two domains have traditionally had little overlap, the Introduction will be aimed more broadly than usual so as to be accessible to audiences from both.

1.1.
Number theory background

The Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture, and its generalizations such as the Beilinson--Bloch Conjecture and Bloch--Kato Conjecture
1
1

1

There is another “Bloch–Kato Conjecture” proved by Voevodsky, which generalizes the Milnor Conjecture about the relationship between Galois cohomology and Milnor K-theory. This is
not
what we are referring to here, although it will become relevant later through the connection to
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-homotopy theory. We will exclusively use the phrase “Norm residue isomorphism” to refer to the Bloch–Kato Conjecture proved by Voevodsky.
, predict a deep relation between algebraic cycles and
L
-functions. (See
[
Liu16
, §1.2]
for a brief introduction to these Conjectures, or
[
BK90
]
for a more extensive reference.) The classical work of Riemann
[
Rie66
]
and Hecke
[
Hec37
]
founded the paradigm of accessing special values of
L
-functions through integral representations as periods of automorphic forms. The work of Gross–Zagier
[
GZ86
]
introduced the idea of accessing the
first derivative
of
L
-functions at special points
2
2

2

In turn, Gross emphasized to us the importance of Stark’s work, in which the derivatives of Artin
L
-functions appear, as an inspiration for
[
GZ86
]
.
as “periods” of geometric incarnations of automorphic forms on arithmetic moduli spaces called Shimura varieties. This opened the door to the rank 1 case of the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer Conjecture; the higher rank case remains wide open.

Theta functions are certain examples of automorphic forms built as generating functions for counting problems associated to lattices. Kudla introduced the concept of
arithmetic theta functions
as an incarnation of theta functions in the arithmetic geometry of Shimura varieties. The so-called
Kudla program
outlined in
[
Kud04
]
(building on
[
Kud97a
,
Kud97b
]
) refers to a strategy to represent the first derivative of standard
L
-functions of cuspidal automorphic representations in terms of arithmetic geometry, giving a higher-dimensional generalization of the Gross–Zagier formula. This program involves several major conjectures:

(1)

The
arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula
, which would relate the arithmetic volumes of arithmetic theta functions to the first derivatives of Siegel–Eisenstein series.

(2)

The
modularity of arithmetic theta functions
, which would enable the construction of arithmetic theta lifts.

(3)

The
arithmetic inner product formula
, which would relate heights of arithmetic theta lifts to the first derivative of standard
L
-functions.

Remarkably, all of these problems have seen major progress in recent years. The works of Li–Zhang
[
LZ20
,
LZ22a
]
, Garcia–Sankaran
[
GS19
]
, and Liu
[
Liu11
]
establish the local arithmetic Siegel-Weil formula for the non-singular Fourier coefficients. The modularity has been proved in many cases; we postpone a more detailed discussion to §
1.3
. The arithmetic inner product formula was proved in many situations by Li–Liu
[
LL21
,
LL22
]
. A modern introduction to these ideas, along with a more complete survey of recent developments, can be found in
[
Li22
]
.

In the function field context, the “higher Gross–Zagier formula” of Yun–Zhang
[
YZ17
,
YZ19
]
revealed the possibility of accessing not only zeroth and first derivatives, but
all higher derivatives
of
L
-functions as periods of geometric incarnations of automorphic forms on moduli spaces of shtukas. In
[
FYZ21a
]
, a
higher Siegel–Weil formula
was established for non-singular terms, constituting the first step of a “higher” version of Kudla’s program in this context. In
[
FYZ21b
]
,
higher theta series
were constructed, and in this paper we prove their generic modularity (following the strategy of
[
FYZ22
]
, which proved the generic modularity of the
\ell
-adic realization). This opens the door to “higher theta-lifting”, of which one possible next application could be a “higher arithmetic inner product formula”, but we view the modularity property as interesting in its own right. We note that the number field analogue of the modularity theorem has already had diverse applications in arithmetic geometry unrelated to the Kudla program.

1.2.
Main result

Let
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be an étale double cover of smooth projective curves over a finite field
\mathbf{F}_{q}
of characteristic
p>2
. Fix integers
n\geqslant m\geq 1
, and
r\geq 0
.

We recall the following definitions from
[
FYZ21b
, §4.5]
:

•

Let
\Bun_{GU^{-}(2m)}
be the moduli stack of triples
(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{M},h)
where
\mathcal{G}
is a vector bundle of rank
2m
over
X^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{M}
is a line bundle over
X
, and
h
is a skew-Hermitian isomorphism

h:\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\sigma^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\vee}\otimes\nu^{*}\mathfrak{M}=\sigma^{*}\mathcal{G}^{*}\otimes\nu^{*}(\omega_{X}\otimes\mathfrak{M}).

•

Let
\Bun_{\widetilde{P}_{m}}
be the moduli stack of quadruples
(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{M},h,\mathcal{E})
where
(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{M},h)\in\Bun_{GU(2m)}
, and
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{G}
is a Lagrangian sub-bundle (of rank
m
).

•

Let
\Sht_{GU(n)}^{r}
be the moduli stack of rank
n
similitude Hermitian shtukas with
r
legs.

In
[
FYZ21b
, §4]
, we constructed the
higher theta series

\widetilde{Z}^{r}_{m}:\Bun_{\widetilde{P}_{m}}(k)\to\CH_{r(n-m)}(\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}),

a function assigning a cycle class on
\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}
to every quadruple
(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{M},h,\mathcal{E})
as above.

1.2.1.
The Modularity Conjecture

The map
\Bun_{\widetilde{P}_{m}}(k)\rightarrow\Bun_{GU(2m)}(k)
, given by forgetting the Lagrangian sub-bundle
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{G}
, is surjective; and
[
FYZ21b
, Modularity Conjecture 4.15]
predicts that
\widetilde{Z}^{r}_{m}
descends through this map to a function
Z^{r}_{m}:\Bun_{GU(2m)}(k)\to\CH_{r(n-m)}(\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)})
, as in the diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{\widetilde{P}_{m}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{GU(2m)}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CH_{r(n-m)}(\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Z}^{r}_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{r}_{m}}

In other words, the Modularity Conjecture says that the function
\widetilde{Z}^{r}_{m}
, which a priori depends on
(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{M},h,\mathcal{E})
, is actually independent of the Lagrangian sub-bundle
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{G}
.

1.2.2.
The generic locus

The stack
\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}
admits a “leg map”
\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}\rightarrow(X^{\prime})^{r}
. Let
\eta=\Spec F^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
be the generic point. Let
\eta^{r}=\Spec(F^{\prime}\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}F^{\prime})\to(X^{\prime})^{r}
. Note that
\eta^{r}
contains the generic point of
(X^{\prime})^{r}
but it also contains many more points such as the generic point of the diagonal
X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow(X^{\prime})^{r}
. We refer to
\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}\times_{(X^{\prime})^{r}}\eta^{r}
as the “generic locus” of
\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}
.

1.2.3.
The generic modularity theorem

We have a restriction map

\CH_{r(n-m)}(\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)})\rightarrow\CH_{r(n-m)}(\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}\times_{(X^{\prime})^{r}}\eta^{r}).

Theorem 1.2.1

(Modularity on the generic locus)
.

The composition

\Bun_{\widetilde{P}_{m}}(k)\xrightarrow{\widetilde{Z}^{r}_{m}}\CH_{r(n-m)}(\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)})\rightarrow\CH_{r(n-m)}(\Sht^{r}_{GU(n)}\times_{(X^{\prime})^{r}}\eta^{r})

descends through
\Bun_{\widetilde{P}_{m}}(k)\twoheadrightarrow\Bun_{GU(2m)}(k)
. In other words, its value on
(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{M},h,\mathcal{E})\in\Bun_{\widetilde{P}_{m}}(k)
is independent of the Lagrangian sub-bundle
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 1.2.2
.

For application to the Kudla program as outlined in §
1.1
, the generic form of modularity established in Theorem
1.2.1
is sufficient for arithmetic theta lifting and the arithmetic inner product formula, according to the paradigm of
[
LL21
,
LL22
]
.

1.3.
Discussion

We discuss some related results to Theorem
1.2.1
. Modularity of arithmetic theta series in the Chow group of Shimura varieties (of the generic fiber), which is analogous to the case
r=1
of Theorem
1.2.1
, is known for unitary Shimura varieties of signature
(n-1,1)
and orthogonal Shimura varieties of signature
(n-2,2)
when the underlying CM field is norm-Euclidean, thanks to work of Borcherds
[
Bor99
]
, Zhang
[
Zha09
]
, and Bruinier–Westerholt-Raum
[
BWR15
]
.
3
3

3

These results were then refined to obtain modularity in the Chow groups of integral models, for unitary Shimura varieties of signature
(n-1,1)
by Bruinier–Howard–Kudla–Rapoport–Yang
[
BHK+20
]
in the divisor case, and for orthogonal Shimura varieties of signature
(n-2,2)
by Howard–Madapusi
[
HM22
]
in all codimensions.
However, the methods behind those results seem completely inapplicable in our situation (there is a brief discussion of this in
[
FYZ22
, §1.2]
), hence the proofs are essentially disjoint, even at the level of ideas.

The cohomological version of Theorem
1.2.1
, modularity of the
\ell
-adic realization on the generic locus, is established in
[
FYZ22
]
, and our proof very much builds on the ideas of
loc. cit.
, as will be discussed further in §
1.4
. In the Shimura variety context, the analogous modularity of the Betti realization was proved much earlier in work of Kudla–Millson
[
KM90
]
, and in complete generality. By contrast, modularity in the Chow group of the generic fiber is more difficult and the known results more restrictive. This is because there are various tools for accessing cohomology, whereas modularity in the Chow group amounts to the fundamentally difficult problem of producing motivic data. The cases in which this has been accessible are limited to those where the Shimura variety supports special cycles that are divisors, for then one can use the (intricate!) theory of Borcherds lifting to write down explicit functions which attest to the necessary relations between divisors. Beyond the case where the Shimura variety supports divisors, nothing seems to be known towards modularity; the motivic data that must be produced is of a more complicated and subtler nature. We note that Kudla has observed in
[
Kud21
]
that assuming (presumably difficult) conjectures of Beilinson–Bloch on the injectivity of Abel–Jacobi maps, and due to an incidental miracle of Hodge diamonds, the modularity in the orthogonal case (on the generic fiber) is implied by modularity in Betti cohomology; Maeda proved an analogous statement in the unitary case
[
Mae22
]
. We do not know if any such phenomenon occurs in the function field context; our proof completely bypasses such questions.

In the function field context there is no analogue of Borcherds lifting, and we do not know any direct way to write down the explicit functions that attest to the necessary relations. We note that the arithmetic theta series live in codimension
mr
, so when
r>1
we are beyond the existence of codimension 1 special cycles, putting us in the realm where the modularity is completely unknown in the Shimura variety context. Nevertheless, our proof produces the necessary relations. Implicitly we are constructing motivic data; for example, for
r=1
and
m=1
our proof implies the existence of certain rational functions on moduli of shtukas that should perhaps be considered as the correct analogues of Borcherds products. But instead of writing down this motivic data explicitly, we produce it as the output of a machine that we call the
motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence
.

It could be interesting to investigate potential applications of the motivic data produced in this way, such as the functions produced in the
m=1,r=1
case, which seem to play the role of Borcherds products on moduli of shtukas. This approach to constructing units is vaguely reminiscent of the approach to modular units and Beilinson–Flach classes via the Manin–Drinfeld Theorem.

1.4.
Commentary on the proof

The proof of Theorem
1.2.1
is patterned on the proof of
[
FYZ22
, Theorem 1.1.1]
, which established modularity after
\ell
-adic realization. In fact we refer the reader to the Introduction and §2.4 of
loc. cit
. for a guide to the strategy of the proof. We will only describe the improvements of this present paper relative to
[
FYZ22
]
.

1.4.1.
Motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence

The classical sheaf-function correspondence is a formalism for extracting functions from sheaves via the trace of Frobenius. One innovation of
[
FYZ22
]
is a “sheaf-cycle” correspondence that extracts the
\ell
-adic realization of cycles from sheaves. More precisely, the strategy of
[
FYZ22
]
is to express the
\ell
-adic realization of higher theta series as the “trace” of a cohomological correspondence between
\ell
-adic sheaves, and then to deduce modularity from some appropriate form of modularity for cohomological correspondences. By definition the trace operation produces elements of Ext groups between
\ell
-adic sheaves, hence can only see
\ell
-adic realizations.

In this paper we upgrade the
\ell
-adic sheaf-cycle correspondence of
[
FYZ22
]
to a
motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence
that directly extracts Chow classes from a suitable notion of motivic sheaves.
Specifically, we will work with the triangulated category of motivic sheaves introduced by V. Voevodsky in the course of his proofs of the Norm Residue isomorphism
4
4

4

This was conjectured by Bloch–Kato, but we avoid calling it the Bloch–Kato Conjecture since it is completely different from the other Bloch–Kato Conjecture which appeared in §
1.1
.
and the Beilinson–Lichtenbaum Conjecture. After further developments by J. Ayoub, D.-C. Cisinski, F. Déglise, F. Morel, and others, we have at our disposal a robust theory of triangulated categories of motivic sheaves over arbitrary base schemes, equipped with Grothendieck’s six operations.
We may regard Voevodsky’s category as the derived category of the hypothetical
abelian
category of perverse motivic sheaves; while Grothendieck’s Standard Conjectures obstruct the existence of this abelian category, or equivalently of the perverse motivic t-structure (see
[
Bei12
]
), Voevodsky’s insight was that the putative derived category can in fact be constructed independently of intractable questions about algebraic cycles. For the purposes of our construction of a motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence, the key property of the derived category of motives is that the Ext groups calculate (higher) Chow groups.

We discuss some differences between motivic sheaves and
\ell
-adic sheaves. In the language of Ayoub
[
Ayo14
]
,
\ell
-adic sheaves are a “transcendental” invariant: they have strong finiteness properties, behave well in families, and are relatively computable; but their relationship to algebraic cycles is tenuous (highly conjectural at best). By contrast, motivic cohomology is what Ayoub calls an “algebro-geometric invariant”, which is built directly out of objects of interest in algebraic geometry (e.g., algebraic cycles), but behaves “chaotically”: it does not have good finiteness properties, it varies violently in families, and it is not amenable to computation. In particular, it is (a priori) ill-defined to form the trace of an endomorphism on motivic cohomology groups, since these groups are usually infinite-dimensional. This presents a challenge for the sheaf-cycle correspondence, which is implemented by formation of trace.

The solution is to expand the meaning of the trace. Dold–Puppe
[
DP80
]
codified the “trace of an endomorphism of a dualizable object in a symmetric monoidal category” as a generalization of the trace in linear algebra. In the category of vector spaces (over a given field), the dualizable objects are precisely the finite-dimensional vector spaces, and the
categorical trace
in the sense of Dold–Puppe is equivalent to the usual trace. However, in a more general symmetric monoidal category, the trace is divorced from its linear algebraic origins, and can be formed without finite-dimensionality conditions. This is precisely how we make sense of the trace for motivic sheaves. We define a motivic version of the Lu–Zheng 2-category from
[
LZ22b
]
, in which
universally strongly locally acyclic
motives form dualizable objects; dualizability provides the analogue of a finiteness property which allows to form the trace even without finite-dimensionality of motivic cohomology. We use Lu–Zheng’s approach to prove a “relative Verdier–Lefschetz formula”, which supplies compatibility of the motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence with proper pushforwards. We also introduce a dual version of the Lu–Zheng category to prove a “relative local term formula”, which supplies compatibility of the motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence with smooth pullbacks; we note that derived algebraic geometry is crucial to formulate the pullback compatibility.

1.4.2.
Motivic Fourier analysis

The modularity of
\ell
-adic cohomological correspondences in
[
FYZ22
]
comes from a derived generalization of Deligne–Laumon’s
\ell
-adic Fourier transform, which allows to execute a sheaf-theoretic version of Poisson’s argument for modularity of the classical theta function. To carry out such arguments, we need to develop a theory of this “derived Fourier analysis” for
motivic sheaves
.

While the
\ell
-adic Fourier transform requires an Artin-Schreier sheaf, hence only exists on spaces in characteristic
p
, there is a variant of Fourier analysis which is more robust, in the sense of being defined in very general geometric and sheaf-theoretic contexts. This variant is based on Laumon’s theory of the
homogeneous Fourier transform

[
Lau03
]
. In anticipation of future applications, we invest effort into developing the homogeneous Fourier transform in great generality in §
8
, encompassing motivic sheaves but also all other known 6-functor formalisms. It turns out that the homogeneous Fourier theory is enough for our applications to the modularity of higher theta functions. (Jakob Scholbach informed us that he is writing up a motivic lift of the Deligne-Laumon Fourier theory; this would also be enough for our applications in the present paper, but at least one of the authors has followup applications in mind that require our more general context.)

We note that, as in
[
FYZ22
]
, we need a
derived
expansion of this theory, which encompasses generalizations of vector bundles called derived vector bundles. This derived generalization presents significant technical difficulties, for which we refer to §
8
and
[
FYZ22
, §6 and Appendix A]
for further discussion.

1.5.
Outline

We give a brief outline of the paper. §
3
establishes some preliminaries on motivic sheaves, especially the notion of universal strong local acyclicity (USLA) and its consequences. Then §
4
– §
7
develop the motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence and the tools to calculate with it. Next §
8
constructs the derived homogeneous Fourier transform and establishes its properties, and §
9
studies its interaction with the motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence. Finally, §
10
assembles everything to prove Theorem
1.2.1
.

2.
Notation and conventions

The notation is consistent with that of
[
FYZ22
]
(and therefore inconsistent with
[
FYZ21a
,
FYZ21b
]
in some ways, as noted there.)

2.1.
Spaces

Unless noted otherwise, we always work in the category of derived Artin stacks. Hence when we say “Cartesian square” we mean what might be called “derived Cartesian square” (sometimes we keep the adjective “derived” for emphasis), unless noted otherwise (the exception is in §
7
).

For a derived Artin stack
A
, we denote by
A_{\mathrm{cl}}
its classical truncation.
By definition, a map of derived Artin stacks is a
closed embedding
or
proper
if the induced map of classical truncations has this property.
For example, the inclusion of the classical truncation
A_{\mathrm{cl}}\hookrightarrow A
is a closed embedding.

2.2.
Perfect complexes

Let
S
be a derived Artin stack.
We let
\Perf(S)
be the
\infty
-category of perfect complexes on
S
(see e.g.
[
GR17
, §3]
).
For
\mathcal{E}\in\Perf(S)
we write
\mathcal{E}^{*}=\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om_{S}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}_{S})
for the linear dual of
\mathcal{E}
.

By a
cochain complex of locally free sheaves
on
S
of amplitude
[a,b]
, we will mean a diagram

\mathcal{E}^{a}\xrightarrow{d^{a}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d^{-2}}\mathcal{E}^{-1}\xrightarrow{d^{-1}}\mathcal{E}^{0}\xrightarrow{d^{0}}\mathcal{E}^{1}\xrightarrow{d^{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d^{b-1}}\mathcal{E}^{b}

in
\Perf(S)
where each
\mathcal{E}^{i}
is of tor-amplitude
[0,0]
, together with null-homotopies
d^{i}\circ d^{i-1}\cong 0
for all
i
.
A
morphism
of cochain complexes
\phi\colon\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}\to\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is a collection of morphisms
\phi^{i}\colon\mathcal{E}^{i}\to\mathcal{F}^{i}
(extending by zero if the complexes are not of the same amplitude), together with a homotopies
\phi^{i+1}\circ d^{i}\cong d^{i}\circ\phi^{i}
as well as compatibilities between the null-homotopies
\phi^{i+1}\circ(d^{i}\circ d^{i-1})\cong 0
and
(d^{i}\circ d^{i-1})\circ\phi^{i-1}\cong 0
.

By taking iterated cofibres, a cochain complex
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}
gives rise to a perfect complex
\mathcal{E}\in\Perf(S)
of tor-amplitude
[a,b]
(note that we are using
cohomological
grading even for tor-amplitude).
Similarly, a morphism of cochain complexes gives rise to a morphism of perfect complexes.
We refer to the cochain complex
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}
as a
global presentation
for
\mathcal{E}
.
More generally, we refer to a diagram of cochain complexes as a global presentation for the induced diagram of perfect complexes.

When
S
is affine, or more generally admits the derived resolution property in the sense of
[
Kha22
, §1.7]
, every perfect complex admits a global presentation.
For a general derived Artin stack
S
, every perfect complex
\mathcal{E}\in\Perf(S)
admits a global presentation smooth-locally on
S
.

2.3.
Cotangent complexes

For a map
f\colon X\to Y
of derived Artin stacks, we denote by
\mathbf{L}_{f}\coloneqq\mathbf{L}_{X/Y}\in\Perf(X)
the relative cotangent complex.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map of derived Artin stacks that is locally finitely presented on classical truncations.
The map
f
is
étale
if the relative cotangent complex
\mathbf{L}_{f}
vanishes (i.e., is isomorphic to
0\in\Perf(X)
).
The map
f
is
smooth
(resp.
quasi-smooth
) if the relative cotangent complex
\mathbf{L}_{f}
is perfect of tor-amplitude
[0,\infty)
(resp.
[-1,\infty)
).

Note that unlike properness, these properties cannot in general be detected on classical truncations.
Moreover, while a smooth map is also smooth on classical truncations, quasi-smoothness is typically destroyed by classical truncation.
See
[
KR19
, §2]
for some background on quasi-smoothness.

When
\mathbf{L}_{f}
is perfect, we write
d(f)
for its virtual rank (or Euler characteristic), and call it the
relative dimension
of
f
.

2.4.
Derived vector bundles

Let
S
be a derived Artin stack.

Given a perfect complex
\mathcal{E}\in\Perf(S)
, we denote by
\Tot(\mathcal{E})
the derived stack of sections of
\mathcal{E}
, as in
[
FYZ22
, §6.1.1]
.
We refer to
\Tot(\mathcal{E})
as the
derived vector bundle
associated with
\mathcal{E}
.
5
5

5

We caution that some other sources (including
[
Kha23
]
) use the dual convention, using Grothendieck’s
\mathbf{V}(-)
construction.

In terms of the functor of points,
\Tot(\mathcal{E})
is the derived stack over
S
sending an
S
-scheme
u:T\to S
to the mapping space
\Map_{\QCoh(T)}(\mathcal{O}_{T},u^{*}\mathcal{E})
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is of tor-amplitude
\geqslant 0
, then we have

\Tot(\mathcal{E})\cong\underline{\smash{\Spec}}_{S}(\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}(\mathcal{E}^{*})).

Thus in that case the projection
\Tot(\mathcal{E})\to S
is affine (but smooth if and only if
\mathcal{E}
is of tor-amplitude
[0,0]
).

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{E}
is of tor-amplitude
\leqslant 0
, then the projection
\Tot(\mathcal{E})\to S
is smooth (but representable if and only if
\mathcal{E}
is of tor-amplitude
[0,0]
).

The
\infty
-category
\operatorname{DVect}(S)
of
derived vector bundles
over
S
is the essential image of the fully faithful functor
\mathcal{E}\mapsto\Tot(\mathcal{E})
from
\Perf(S)
to the
\infty
-category of derived stacks over
S
with
\mathbf{G}_{m}
-action.

Throughout we use calligraphic letters such as
\mathcal{E}
for perfect complexes, and Roman letters such as
E
for the corresponding total spaces. We will denote the dual derived vector bundle to
E=\Tot(\mathcal{E})
by
\widehat{E}=\Tot(\mathcal{E}^{*})
.

Using the equivalence
\Tot(-)\colon\Perf(S)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{DVect}(S)
, we can make sense of global presentations of (diagrams of) derived vector bundles just as in §
2.2
.
That is, a global presentation for
E=\Tot(\mathcal{E})
is a global presentation for
\mathcal{E}\in\Perf(S)
.

2.5.
\infty
-categories

In an
\infty
-category
\mathsf{C}
, we use the notation
\Map(c,c^{\prime})
for the mapping space between objects
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}
. We use the notation
\Hom(c,c^{\prime})\coloneqq\pi_{0}\Map(c,c^{\prime})
, which is the group of morphisms from
c
to
c^{\prime}
in the homotopy category of
\mathsf{C}
. We denote
\Ext^{i}(c,c^{\prime})\coloneqq\Hom(c,c^{\prime}[i])
.

2.6.
Motives

We refer to §
3.1
for the precise definition of motivic sheaves adopted in this paper, and then §
3.2
for additional relevant notation.

3.
Motivic sheaf theory

In this section we establish some general material on motivic sheaves and motivic cohomology. We define the notion of
(universally) strongly locally acyclic
motivic sheaves and their properties; this part is similar to work of Jin
[
Jin24
]
which is itself a motivic version of work of Lu–Zheng
[
LZ22b
]
. However, these earlier works focus on the case of schemes while for applications we need the generality of derived Artin stacks, so we formulate the statements in this generality, and give proofs when they need to be modified from the case of schemes.

3.1.
The derived category of motives

For a derived Artin stack
S
, we have the stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
of motivic sheaves on
S
with rational coefficients.

Recall that for a scheme
S
, the motivic stable homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(S)
along with the six-functor formalism for the assignment
S\mapsto\mathrm{SH}(S)
was constructed by Morel and Voevodsky
[
Voe98
,
MV99
,
Del01
]
and developed further by Ayoub
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
and Cisinski–Déglise
[
CD19
]
(see also
[
Hoy15
, App. C]
or
[
Kha21
]
for non-noetherian bases).
The six-functor formalism descends to the étale-localized and rationalized categories
\mathrm{SH}_{\et}(S;\mathbf{Q})
, and we take
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({-};\mathbf{Q})\coloneqq\mathrm{SH}_{\et}(-;\mathbf{Q})
by definition on schemes.
6
6

6

Since some older references operate with triangulated categories or model categories, we clarify that we will always use the
\infty
-categorical incarnation of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
.
See e.g.
[
Kha21
]
for the construction of the six operations at the
\infty
-categorical level.

This is also known as Ayoub’s category
\mathrm{DA}_{\et}({S};\mathbf{Q})
of étale motives with rational coefficients (see
[
Ayo14
]
for an introduction).
This category has been defined and studied in various other guises, which are described and compared in
[
CD19
]
:

•

(Beilinson motives) By
[
CD19
, Theorem 16.2.13]
,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
is equivalent to the category of
Beilinson motives
over
S
in the sense of
[
CD19
, §14]
.
In particular, if
S
is noetherian and finite-dimensional, then by
[
CD16
, Theorem 5.2.2]

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
is equivalent to the category
\DM_{h}(S;\mathbf{Q})
of
h
-motives
(with rational coefficients).

•

(Morel motives) By
[
CD19
, Theorem 16.2.18]
,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
is equivalent to the category of
Morel motives
over
S
in the sense of
[
CD19
, §16.2]
.

•

(Voevodsky motives) If
S
is excellent and geometrically unibranch, then by
[
CD19
, Theorem 16.1.4]

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
is equivalent to the category
\DM(S;\mathbf{Q})
of
Voevodsky motives
over
S
(with rational coefficients).

•

(
H\mathbf{Q}
-linear motivic spectra) For a commutative ring
\Lambda
, let
H\Lambda_{S}\in\mathrm{SH}(S)
denote the
\Lambda
-linear motivic Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum as defined in
[
Spi18
]
.
For
\Lambda=\mathbf{Q}
,
H\mathbf{Q}_{S}
is isomorphic to the Beilinson motivic cohomology spectrum of
[
CD19
, Definition 14.1.2]
by
[
Spi18
, Theorem 7.14]
.
In particular, by
[
CD19
, Theorem 14.2.9]
, the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{D}_{H\Lambda}(S)
of modules over
H\Lambda_{S}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of Beilinson motives over
S
, and hence to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
.

The generalization of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({-};\mathbf{Q})
to derived algebraic spaces and derived Artin stacks is developed in
[
Kha19b
, Appendix A]
. To explicate this, we remark that
\mathrm{SH}(S)
and hence
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
is invariant under passing to the classical truncation
S_{\mathrm{cl}}
by
[
Kha19a
]
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({-};\mathbf{Q})
is extended from derived schemes to derived Artin stacks by right Kan extension. Explicitly, this means that if
S
is a derived Artin stack then

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})=\varprojlim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T};\mathbf{Q})

where the limit is over the category of smooth morphisms
T\rightarrow S
from derived schemes
T
.
If
T\twoheadrightarrow S
is a smooth
atlas
from a derived scheme, then
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
agrees with the category of Cartesian sheaves on the simplicial derived scheme
T_{\bullet}=\{T\times_{S}\ldots\times_{S}T\}
.
The six-functor formalism also extends to derived Artin stacks by
[
Kha19b
, Thm. A.5]
.

3.2.
Notations for motives

For a derived Artin stack
A
, we denote by
\mathbf{Q}_{A}
(or just
\mathbf{Q}
if the context is clear) the unit of the symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
.

The category
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({-};\mathbf{Q})
contains a “Tate motive”
\mathbf{Q}(1)
. For
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
, we write
\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle i\rangle}\coloneqq\mathcal{K}[2i](i)
for the indicated shift and Tate twist.

For
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
, we abbreviate

\Hom_{A}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\coloneqq\Hom_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}).

For a map
f\colon A\rightarrow S
of derived Artin stacks, we denote by
\mathbf{D}_{A/S}(-)
the relative Verdier dual functor,

\mathbf{D}_{A/S}(\mathcal{K})\coloneqq\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om_{A}(\mathcal{K},f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{S}).

We also abbreviate
\mathbf{D}_{A/S}\coloneqq\mathbf{D}_{A/S}(\mathbf{Q}_{A})
for the relative dualizing complex of
f
.

3.3.
Geometric motives

Given a smooth map of derived Artin stacks
f\colon T\rightarrow S
, there is a functor

f_{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})

which is
left
adjoint to the pullback
f^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T};\mathbf{Q})
. If
S
is a derived scheme, the subcategory
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({S};\mathbf{Q})\subset\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
of
geometric motives
is the thick subcategory generated by
f_{\sharp}\mathbf{Q}_{T}{\scriptstyle\langle i\rangle}
as
f\colon T\rightarrow S
ranges over smooth morphisms of derived schemes and
i
ranges over all integers.

If
S
is a derived stack, then we say that a motive
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
is
geometric
if it is geometric after pullback to some (equivalently, any) atlas
S^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow S
where
S^{\prime}
is a derived scheme. We denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({S};\mathbf{Q})\subset\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
the full subcategory of geometric motives.

Example 3.3.1
.

For any derived Artin stack
A
, the unit
\mathbf{Q}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
is geometric.

Remark 3.3.2

(Preservation under six functors)
.

For
f\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
a map of derived schemes of finite type over a quasi-excellent scheme, the property of being geometric is preserved by the functors
f_{!},f_{*},f^{*},f^{!}
(see
[
CD19
, Theorem 15.2.1]
). It then follows that for a map
f\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
of derived Artin stacks locally of finite type over a quasi-excellent scheme, geometricity is preserved by the functors
f^{*}
and
f^{!}
; and geometricity is preserved by the functors
f_{!}
and
f_{*}

if

f
is representable in derived schemes.

Finally, we note that
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om(-,-)
and
-\otimes-
preserve geometric motives on schemes, and are compatible with smooth base change, hence they preserve geometric motives on derived Artin stacks.

3.4.
The effective homotopy t-structure

For a derived scheme
S
, let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})^{\leqslant 0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
denote the full subcategory generated under colimits and extensions by objects of the form
a_{!}a^{!}(\mathbf{Q}_{S})
, for
a\colon X\to S
a smooth morphism from a scheme.
This forms the connective part of the
effective homotopy t-structure
on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
(see
[
BH21
, Sect. 13, App. B]
).
The coconnective part
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})^{\geqslant 0}
is thus spanned by those
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
for which the groups

\mathrm{H}^{-n}(X;\mathcal{K})\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})}(\mathbf{Q}_{S}[n],a_{*}a^{*}(\mathcal{K}))\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})}(a_{!}a^{!}(\mathbf{Q}_{S})[n],\mathcal{K})

vanish for all
n>0
and all smooth morphisms
a\colon X\to S
with
X
a scheme.

For a derived Artin stack
S
, we say that an object
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
belongs to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})^{\leqslant 0}
, resp.
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})^{\geqslant 0}
, if
u^{*}(\mathcal{K})
belongs to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({U};\mathbf{Q})^{\leqslant 0}
, resp.
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({U};\mathbf{Q})^{\geqslant 0}
for some smooth atlas
u\colon U\twoheadrightarrow S
.
The proof of
[
KR22
, Prop. 5.3]
applies verbatim to show that this defines a t-structure on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

For every derived Artin stack
S
locally of finite type over a field
k
, the unit
\mathbf{Q}_{S}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})
belongs to the heart of the effective homotopy t-structure.

Proof.

We may assume that
S
is a scheme.
We have
\mathbf{Q}_{S}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S};\mathbf{Q})^{\leqslant 0}
by definition, so it remains to show that for every scheme
Y
which is smooth over
S
, the spectrum

R\Gamma(Y,\mathbf{Q}_{Y})

is connective, i.e.,
\mathrm{H}^{-n}(Y;\mathbf{Q}_{Y})\cong 0
for
n>0
.
For any prime
\ell\neq\operatorname{char}(k)
, there exists by de Jong–Gabber an
\ell
dh-hypercover
Y^{\prime}_{\bullet}\to Y
where each
Y^{\prime}_{n}
is a regular scheme.
Since motivic cohomology with rational coefficients satisfies
\ell
dh descent by
[
Gei14
, Thm. 1.2]
, and connectivity is stable under limits, we may assume that
Y
is regular.
In this case we have

\mathrm{H}^{-n}(Y;\mathbf{Q})\cong\mathrm{H}^{-n}(Y;\mathrm{KGL}_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(0)})\cong\Gr^{0}_{\gamma}(\operatorname{K}_{n}(Y)_{\mathbf{Q}})

where
\operatorname{K}_{n}(Y)_{\mathbf{Q}}\coloneqq\pi_{n}(\operatorname{K}(Y))\otimes\mathbf{Q}
and
\operatorname{K}(Y)
is the algebraic K-theory spectrum of
Y
(see
[
CD19
, §14.1]
).
But
\operatorname{Fil}^{1}_{\gamma}\operatorname{K}_{n}(Y)_{\mathbf{Q}}=\operatorname{K}_{n}(Y)_{\mathbf{Q}}
holds for
n>0
by definition of the augmented
\lambda
-ring structure on
\operatorname{K}_{n}(Y)_{\mathbf{Q}}
(see e.g.
[
Wei13
, IV, §5, p. 345]
).
∎

3.5.
Chow groups as motivic Borel-Moore homology

Let
A
be a derived Artin stack locally of finite type over a field
\mathbf{F}
.

We define the
Chow groups
of
A
(with rational coefficients) by

\CH_{i}(A)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{-2i}(A;\pi^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{\Spec(F)}(-i))\cong\mathrm{H}^{0}(A;\pi^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{\Spec(F)}\langle{-i}\rangle),\quad\text{ for }i\in\mathbf{Z}

where
\pi\colon A\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbf{F})
is the structural morphism.
This definition agrees with the rationalization of the classical definition of Chow groups
under assumptions
that
A
is ‘‘reasonable’’.
7
7

7

Our point of view is that when
A
is unreasonable, then our definition of
\CH_{i}(A)
is the “correct” one, being well-behaved from various technical perspectives.
More precisely, according to
[
Kha19b
, Example 2.10]
, when
A
is a classical
1
-Artin stack of finite type over
k
with affine stabilizers, this recovers the Chow group (with
\mathbf{Q}
-coefficients) of Kresch
[
Kre99
]
. If
A
is a derived Artin stack, then by the derived invariance of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
, the inclusion of the classical truncation
A_{\mathrm{cl}}\hookrightarrow A
induces isomorphisms
\CH_{i}(A_{\mathrm{cl}})\cong\CH_{i}(A)
.

We define the
Chow cohomology groups
of
A
(with rational coefficients) by

\CH^{i}(A)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{2i}(A;\mathbf{Q}_{A}(i))\cong\mathrm{H}^{0}(A;\mathbf{Q}_{A}\langle{i}\rangle),\quad\text{ for }i\in\mathbf{Z}.

We caution that these map to, but are typically
not
the same as, Fulton’s operational Chow cohomology groups
[
Ful98
, §17]
, even for classical quasi-projective schemes (unless
A
is smooth).

More generally, for a locally of finite type morphism
f\colon A\rightarrow B
of derived Artin stacks over a field
\mathbf{F}
, we define the
relative Chow groups of
f
to be

\CH_{i}(A/B)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{-2i}(A;f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B}(-i))\cong\mathrm{H}^{0}(A;f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B}\langle{-i}\rangle),\quad\text{for }i\in\mathbf{Z}.

We have
\CH_{i}(A/A)\simeq\CH^{-i}(A)
for all
i
.
Again, these are a refinement of the operational or bivariant Chow groups of
[
Ful98
, §17]
.

3.6.
Functoriality of Chow groups

3.6.1.
Proper pushforward

The Chow groups are covariantly functorial with respect to proper morphisms.
That is, if
f\colon A\to B
is a proper morphism of derived Artin stacks, then we have pushforward maps

(3.6.1)

f_{*}\colon\CH_{i}(A)\to\CH_{i}(B)

and more generally
f_{*}\colon\CH_{i}(A/C)\to\CH_{i}(B/C)
if
f
is defined over some
C
.
In terms of the six functors, these are induced by the natural transformation
f_{*}f^{!}\to\mathrm{id}
, counit of the adjunction
(f_{*},f^{!})
.

3.6.2.
Gysin pullback

Let
f\colon A\rightarrow B
be a quasi-smooth map of derived Artin stacks, of relative dimension
d(f)
.
There are (virtual) Gysin pullback maps

(3.6.2)

f^{!}\colon\CH_{i}(B)\to\CH_{i+d(f)}(A),

and more generally
f^{!}:\CH_{i}(B/C)\to\CH_{i+d(f)}(A/C)
if
B
is defined over
C
.
These are functorial and satisfy a base change formula with respect to proper pushforwards.

The maps (
3.6.2
) are induced by a natural transformation

(3.6.3)

\mathrm{gys}_{f}\colon f^{*}\rightarrow f^{!}{\scriptstyle\langle-d(f)\rangle}

called the Gysin transformation, constructed in
[
Kha19b
, §3]
.
It satisfies various natural compatibilities detailed in
[
Kha19b
, §3.2]
or
[
FYZ22
, §3.4]
.
For example, when
f
is smooth, the Gysin transformation recovers the Poincaré duality isomorphism
f^{*}\cong f^{!}{\scriptstyle\langle-d(f)\rangle}
.

In particular, one has a relative (virtual) fundamental class

(3.6.4)

[A/B]\coloneqq[f]\in\CH_{d(f)}(A/B)

defined as the Gysin pullback of the unit in
\CH_{0}(B/B)\simeq\CH^{0}(B)
.
Equivalently, it is determined by the morphism

(3.6.5)

\mathbf{Q}_{A}\cong f^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{B}\xrightarrow{[f]}f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B}{\scriptstyle\langle-d(f)\rangle}

obtained by evaluating the Gysin transformation (
3.6.3
) on
\mathbf{Q}_{B}
.

3.7.
USLA motives

Let
S
be a derived Artin stack locally of finite type over a field.

Definition 3.7.1
.

Let
f\colon A\rightarrow S
be a map of derived Artin stacks. Following
[
Jin24
, Definition 3.1.1]
, we say that
\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
is
strongly locally acyclic
(SLA) over
S
if for any schematic map of derived Artin stacks
g\colon T\rightarrow S
, inducing the Cartesian square

(3.7.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and any
\mathcal{K}_{T}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T};\mathbf{Q})
, the canonical map

(3.7.2)

\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}\rightarrow g^{\prime}_{*}((g^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})

is an isomorphism.

We say that
\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
is
universally strongly locally acyclic
(USLA) over
S
if for any morphism
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, the
*
-pullback of
\mathcal{K}_{A}
to
S^{\prime}\times_{S}A^{\prime}
is SLA over
S^{\prime}
.

The property of being (U)SLA can be checked locally in the smooth topology on the source and target.

Lemma 3.7.2
.

Maintain the notation of Definition
3.7.1
.

(1) Let
h\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
be a smooth, surjective morphism of derived Artin stacks. Then
\mathcal{K}_{A}
is (U)SLA over
S
if and only if
h^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}
is (U)SLA over
S
.

(2) Let
h\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
be a smooth, surjective morphism of derived Artin stacks. Let
h_{A}
be the base change of
h
to
A
. Then
\mathcal{K}_{A}
is (U)SLA over
S
if and only if
h^{*}_{A}\mathcal{K}_{A}
is (U)SLA over
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(1) Since
h
is surjective, (
3.7.2
) is an isomorphism if and only if

(3.7.3)

h^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})\rightarrow h^{*}g^{\prime}_{*}((g^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})

is an isomorphism. Given a schematic map
g\colon T\rightarrow S
of derived Artin stacks, we have a commutative diagram

(3.7.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where all squares are derived Cartesian and
h,h^{\prime}
are smooth. Using smooth base change, this induces a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}g^{\prime}_{*}((g^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes h^{*}f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}_{*}(h^{\prime})^{*}((g^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A})\otimes(f\circ h)^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}_{*}((g^{\prime\prime})^{*}(h^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A})\otimes(f^{\prime}\circ h^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

and comparing the top and bottom rows shows that (
3.7.3
) is equivalent to
h^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}
being SLA over
S
. Running the same argument over all base changes shows that
\mathcal{K}_{A}
is USLA over
S
if and only if
h^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}
is USLA over
S
.

(2) The argument is similar.
∎

Example 3.7.3
.

If
S
is a point (by which we mean the spectrum of a field) then every object of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
is USLA over
S
. Indeed, if
A
is a derived scheme this follows from
[
JY21
, 2.1.14]
, and the general case follows by induction and cohomological descent.

The (U)SLA property is preserved by direct image along proper morphisms.

Lemma 3.7.4
.

Let
h\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
be a proper morphism of derived Artin stacks over
S
. Let
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A^{\prime}};\mathbf{Q})
be (U)SLA over
S
. Then
h_{!}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
is (U)SLA over
S
.

Proof.

By proper base change, it suffices to show that
f_{!}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}
is SLA over
S
if
\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}
is SLA over
S
. Consider the commutative diagram (
3.7.4
)
where all squares are derived Cartesian and
h,h^{\prime}
are proper. We want to show that the map

h_{!}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}\rightarrow g^{\prime}_{*}((g^{\prime})^{*}h_{!}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})

is an isomorphism. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{*}((g^{\prime})^{*}h_{!}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{*}(h^{\prime}_{!}(g^{\prime\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}_{!}((g^{\prime\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes(h^{\prime})^{*}(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}(\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes h^{*}f^{*}g_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{!}g^{\prime\prime}_{*}((g^{\prime\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}\otimes(h^{\prime})^{*}(f^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
proper base change
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{$\scriptstyle h$ proper }\implies}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
projection formula
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
projection formula

The bottom horizontal map is an isomorphism by definition of
\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\prime}
being SLA over
S
, hence so is the top horizontal map.
∎

3.8.
Relative Künneth formulae

Let
S
be a derived Artin stack.

Notation 3.8.1
.

Let
A_{0},A_{1}
be derived stacks over
S
and
\mathcal{K}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A_{0}};\mathbf{Q})
,
\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A_{1}};\mathbf{Q})
. We write

\mathcal{K}_{0}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\pr_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\otimes\pr_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A_{0}\times_{S}A_{1}};\mathbf{Q}).

Lemma 3.8.2
.

Let
f_{0}\colon A_{0}\rightarrow B_{0}
and
f_{1}\colon A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1}
be locally finite type morphisms of derived Artin stacks over
S
.
Then the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}\times_{S}A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}\times_{S}B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}\times_{S}f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

induces an isomorphism

(3.8.1)

f_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0}\boxtimes_{S}f_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}(f_{0}\times_{S}f_{1})_{!}(\mathcal{K}_{0}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{1}),

natural in
\mathcal{K}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A_{0}};\mathbf{Q})
and
\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A_{1}};\mathbf{Q})
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
JY21
, Lemma 2.2.3]
works verbatim.
∎

Suppose we have a commutative diagram of derived Artin stacks

(3.8.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

There is a natural map

(3.8.3)

f_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\rightarrow(f\times_{S}\Id_{A})_{*}(\mathcal{K}_{T}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S^{\prime}\times_{S}A};\mathbf{Q})

defined by adjunction from the composition of maps

(f\times_{S}\Id_{A})^{*}(f_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})\cong f^{*}f_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{counit}}\mathcal{K}_{T}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T\times_{S}A};\mathbf{Q}).

Lemma 3.8.3
.

Let notation be as in diagram (
3.8.2
). Let
\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
be USLA over
S
.

(1) If
f\colon T\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is schematic, then for any
\mathcal{K}_{T}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T};\mathbf{Q})
, the canonical morphism

f_{*}\mathcal{K}_{T}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\xrightarrow{(\ref{eq: relative projection *})}(f\times_{S}\Id_{A})_{*}(\mathcal{K}_{T}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S^{\prime}\times_{S}A};\mathbf{Q})

is an isomorphism.

(2) If
f\colon T\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is locally of finite type, then for any
\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S^{\prime}};\mathbf{Q})
, the natural morphism (adjoint to (
3.8.1
))

f^{!}\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\rightarrow(f\times_{S}\Id_{A})^{!}(\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T\times_{S}A};\mathbf{Q})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof is the essentially the same as that of
[
Jin24
, Lemma 3.1.4]
.

(1) This is exactly the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{A}
being SLA after base change along
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
.

(2) The statement can be checked smooth-locally on
T
.
Thus we may assume
f
is schematic and moreover factors through a closed immersion and a smooth morphism. If
f
is smooth, then the result follows from the Poincaré duality isomorphism §
3.6
and its compatibility with base change. If
f
is a closed embedding, then write
j\colon U\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
for the complementary open. Abbreviate
f_{A}\coloneqq(f\times\Id_{A})
and
j_{A}\coloneqq(j\times\Id_{A})
. We have a map of excision sequences in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T\times_{S}A};\mathbf{Q})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{!}\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{*}\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{*}j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}^{!}(\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}^{*}j_{A*}j_{A}^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{S^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})}

The middle vertical map is obviously an isomorphism. The right vertical map is an isomorphism by item (1) applied to
U\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
. Therefore the left vertical map is an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 3.8.4
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
be USLA over
S
. Then for every derived Artin stack
T
over
S
, the canonical map

\mathbf{D}_{T/S}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\rightarrow\pr_{A}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({T\times_{S}A};\mathbf{Q})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
3.8.3
(2) with
S^{\prime}=S
,
f
the morphism
T\rightarrow S
, and
\mathcal{K}_{S}=\mathbf{Q}_{S}
.
∎

Proposition 3.8.5
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({B};\mathbf{Q})
be USLA over
S
and
\mathcal{K}_{B}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({B};\mathbf{Q})
. Then
the canonical morphism

(3.8.4)

(\mathbf{D}_{B/S}\mathcal{K}_{B})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om_{B\times_{S}A}(\pr_{B}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{B},\pr_{A}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{A})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By smooth base change, the map (
3.8.4
) can be checked to be an isomorphism smooth locally on
A
and
B
. Since the hypotheses are also stable under smooth base change (using §
3.7.2
), we may assume that
A
and
B
are schemes. By the definition of geometric motives, it suffices to check this on for
\mathcal{K}_{B}
of the form
f_{\sharp}\mathbf{Q}_{T}
for smooth
f\colon T\rightarrow B
(since the statement is evidently compatible with shifts and Tate twists). We refer to the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Using that
f_{\sharp}\mathbf{Q}_{T}\cong f_{!}f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B}\cong f_{!}\mathbf{Q}_{T}{\scriptstyle\langle d(f)\rangle}
, proper base change gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om_{B\times_{S}A}(\pr_{B}^{*}f_{\sharp}\mathbf{Q}_{T},\pr_{A}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{A})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om_{B\times_{S}A}(f_{A!}\pr_{T}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{T}{\scriptstyle\langle d(f)\rangle},\pr_{A}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{A})

\displaystyle\cong f_{A*}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om_{T\times_{S}A}(\pr_{T}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{T}{\scriptstyle\langle d(f)\rangle},f_{A}^{!}\pr_{A}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{A})

where we write
f_{A}\colon T\times_{S}A\rightarrow B\times_{S}A
for the pullback of
f
. Since
\mathcal{K}_{A}
is assumed to be USLA over
S
, from Corollary
3.8.4
we have

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}om_{T\times_{S}A}(\pr_{T}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{T}{\scriptstyle\langle d(f)\rangle},f_{A}^{!}\pr_{A}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{A})\cong\mathbf{D}_{T/S}(f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}.

Again since
\mathcal{K}_{A}
is USLA over
S
, Lemma
3.8.3
(1) applies to give

f_{A*}(\mathbf{D}_{T/S}(f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A})\cong(f_{*}\mathbf{D}_{T/S}f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A}\cong\mathbf{D}_{B/S}(f_{!}f^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{B})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{A},

as desired.
∎

4.
Cohomological correspondences

In this section we establish some general material related to cohomological correspondences. In §
4.2
, we recall the notion of “pushable” and “pullable” squares from
[
FYZ22
, §4, §5]
and the base change natural transformations that they entail. In §
4.1
we recall the notion of cohomological correspondence, and in §
4.3
we formulate the notion of pushforward and pullback for cohomological correspondences. Finally in §
4.4
we state the Base Change Theorem for cohomological correspondences. The constructions and proofs carry over verbatim from
\ell
-adic sheaves as considered in
[
FYZ22
, §3, §4]
to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({-};\mathbf{Q})
, so we just formulate the statements without proof.

4.1.
Cohomological correspondences

Let
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
be derived Artin stacks. A
correspondence between
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
is a diagram of derived Artin stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

where
c_{1}
is locally of finite type.
A
map of correspondences
from
(A_{0}\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}A_{1})
to
(B_{0}\xleftarrow{d_{0}}D\xrightarrow{d_{1}}B_{1})
is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

Let
\mathcal{K}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A_{0}};\mathbf{Q})
,
\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A_{1}};\mathbf{Q})
. A
cohomological correspondence from
\mathcal{K}_{0}
to
\mathcal{K}_{1}
supported on
C
is a map

c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({C};\mathbf{Q})
. The vector space of such is denoted

(4.1.1)

\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\coloneqq\Hom_{C}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}).

4.1.1.
Fixed points of a self-correspondence

Suppose that we have a fixed isomorphism
A_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{1}
, which we will sometimes use to identify
A_{0}
with
A_{1}
; however, it will also be convenient to distinguish them at times. Let
\Delta\colon A_{0}\rightarrow A_{0}\times A_{1}
be the diagonal embedding. Define
\Fix(C)
as the fibered product

(4.1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}\times A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

where
c=(c_{0},c_{1})
.

4.2.
Base change transformations

In order to discuss the functoriality of cohomological correspondences, we make a brief detour on base change transformations.

4.2.1.
Pushable and pullable squares

The notions of
pushable
and
pullable
squares were defined in
[
FYZ22
, Definition 3.1.1]
in order to codify situations where base change natural transformations can be constructed. Later we realized that the notion of pushable square appears at least implicitly in
[
Zhe15
]
for the same reason. We repeat the definitions for the convenience of the reader.

Let

(4.2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

be a commutative square of derived Artin stacks.
Denote by
\widetilde{B}=C\times_{D}B
the derived fibered product so that the square (
4.2.1
) decomposes into a commutative diagram

(4.2.2)

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{D}

where the bottom right square is derived Cartesian.

Definition 4.2.1
.

The square (
4.2.1
) is called

•

pushable
, if
a
is proper.

•

pullable
, if
a
is quasi-smooth. In this case, the
defect
of the square is by definition the relative dimension
d(a)
.

Remark 4.2.2
.

Note that pushability is a purely topological notion: it can be checked on classical truncations (and even on underlying reduced stacks). By contrast, pullability is sensitive to the derived structure, and most of the pullable squares that arise for us would not be pullable on classical truncations.

Example 4.2.3
.

If
f
is separated and
f^{\prime}
is proper, then (
4.2.2
) is pushable. If
f
is smooth and
f^{\prime}
is quasi-smooth, then (
4.2.2
) is pullable.

Remark 4.2.4
.

We will also have occasion to consider the following variant: we say (
4.2.1
) is
topologically pullable
if the morphism
a
is a finite radicial surjection (e.g. if it is an isomorphism on reduced classical truncations).
In this case the defect is zero by convention.

4.2.2.
Proper base change

Suppose (
4.2.1
) is Cartesian after taking classical truncations and then underlying reduced stacks. Then there is a proper base change natural isomorphism

(4.2.3)

g^{*}f_{!}\xrightarrow{\diamond}f^{\prime}_{!}(g^{\prime})^{*}

of functors
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({B};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({C};\mathbf{Q})
which we label by “
\diamond
”. We use the same notation for the natural isomorphism

f^{\prime}_{*}(g^{\prime})^{!}\xrightarrow{\diamond}g^{!}f_{*}.

By adjunction, (
4.2.3
) induces natural transformations

f_{!}(g^{\prime})_{*}\xrightarrow{\diamond}g_{*}f^{\prime}_{!}

and

(g^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}\xrightarrow{\diamond}(f^{\prime})^{!}g^{*}

which we will also label by “
\diamond
”.

4.2.3.
Push-pull base change transformation

Suppose (
4.2.1
) is pushable. Then we have a natural transformation of functors
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({B};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({C};\mathbf{Q})

(4.2.4)

g^{*}f_{!}\xrightarrow{\triangledown}f^{\prime}_{!}(g^{\prime})^{*}

defined as the composition

g^{*}f_{!}\xrightarrow{\diamond}\widetilde{f}_{!}\widetilde{g}^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{unit}(a)}\widetilde{f}_{!}a_{*}a^{*}\widetilde{g}^{*}=\widetilde{f}_{!}a_{!}a^{*}\widetilde{g}^{*}=f^{\prime}_{!}(g^{\prime})^{*}

Here we used that
\mathrm{fsupp}_{a}\colon a_{!}\rightarrow a_{*}
is invertible because
a
is proper. We sometimes denote this base change transformation by
\triangledown
.

4.2.4.
Push-push base change transformations

Suppose (
4.2.1
) is pushable. Then we have a natural transformation of functors
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({D};\mathbf{Q})

(4.2.5)

f_{!}g^{\prime}_{*}\to g_{*}f^{\prime}_{!}

defined as the composition

f_{!}g^{\prime}_{*}=f_{!}\widetilde{g}_{*}a_{*}\xrightarrow{\diamond}g_{*}\widetilde{f}_{!}a_{*}=g_{*}\widetilde{f}_{!}a_{!}\to g_{*}f^{\prime}_{!}.

Again we used that
\mathrm{fsupp}_{a}\colon a_{!}\rightarrow a_{*}
is invertible because
a
is proper. We sometimes denote this base change transformation by
\triangledown
.

4.2.5.
Pull-pull base change transformation

Suppose that (
4.2.1
) is pullable with defect
\delta
. Then we have a natural transformation of functors
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({D};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})

(4.2.6)

(f^{\prime})^{*}g^{!}\xrightarrow{\vartriangle}(g^{\prime})^{!}f^{*}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta\rangle}

defined as the composition

(f^{\prime})^{*}g^{!}=a^{*}\widetilde{f}^{*}g^{!}\xrightarrow{a^{*}\diamond}a^{*}\widetilde{g}^{!}f^{*}\xrightarrow{[a]}a^{!}\widetilde{g}^{!}f^{*}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta\rangle}=(g^{\prime})^{!}f^{*}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta\rangle}.

We often denote such a natural transformation induced by a pullable square by
\vartriangle
.

Remark 4.2.5
.

If (
4.2.1
) is pullable, the map (
4.2.6
) induces by adjunction a map

(4.2.7)

g^{\prime}_{!}(f^{\prime})^{*}\xrightarrow{\vartriangle}f^{*}g_{!}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta\rangle}.

Remark 4.2.6
.

If (
4.2.1
) is topologically pullable as in Remark
4.2.4
, then we have a canonical isomorphism
a^{*}\cong a^{!}
by topological invariance (see
[
EK20
, Remark 2.1.13]
).
Therefore we may define a natural transformation
\vartriangle\colon(f^{\prime})^{*}g^{!}\to(g^{\prime})^{!}f^{*}
just as in (
4.2.6
).

4.2.6.
Compatibility with compositions

The natural transformations
\triangledown
and
\vartriangle
are compatible with compositions in the following sense. Suppose we have a commutative diagram

(4.2.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

According to
[
FYZ22
, Lemma 3.2.2]
and
[
FYZ22
, Lemma 3.5.3]
:

(a)

If both the upper square and the lower square are pushable, then the outer square formed by
(A,B,E,F)
is also pushable.

(b)

If both up the upper square and the lower square are pullable, say of defects
\delta_{\textup{upp}}
and
\delta_{\textup{low}}
, then the outer square is also pullable, with defect
\delta_{\textup{out}}\coloneqq\delta_{\textup{upp}}+\delta_{\textup{low}}
.

Suppose the outer and lower squares in (
4.2.8
) are pushable. Then by the same argument as in proof of
[
FYZ22
, Proposition 3.2.3]
, we have the following commutative diagrams of natural transformations
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({B};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({E};\mathbf{Q})
, resp.
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({F};\mathbf{Q})
:

(4.2.9)

\textstyle{g^{*}h_{!}f_{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\triangledown f_{!}}
\textstyle{h^{\prime}_{!}(g^{\prime})^{*}f_{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}_{!}\triangledown}
\textstyle{h^{\prime}_{!}f^{\prime}_{!}(g^{\prime\prime})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{g^{*}(h\circ f)_{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\triangledown}
\textstyle{(h^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime})_{!}(g^{\prime\prime})^{*}}

\textstyle{h_{!}f_{!}g^{\prime\prime}_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{!}\triangledown}
\textstyle{h_{!}g^{\prime}_{*}f^{\prime}_{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\triangledown f^{\prime}_{!}}
\textstyle{g_{*}h^{\prime}_{!}f^{\prime}_{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(h\circ f)_{!}g^{\prime\prime}_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\triangledown}
\textstyle{g_{*}(h^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime})_{!}}

Suppose the upper and lower squares in (
4.2.8
) are pullable, of defects
\delta_{\textup{upp}}
and
\delta_{\textup{low}}
. Then by the same argument as in proofs of
[
FYZ22
, Proposition 3.5.4]
, we have the following commutative diagram of natural transformations
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({F};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
:

(4.2.10)

\textstyle{(f^{\prime})^{*}(h^{\prime})^{*}g^{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}\vartriangle}
\textstyle{(f^{\prime})^{*}(g^{\prime})^{!}h^{*}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\textup{low}}\rangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartriangle h^{*}}
\textstyle{(g^{\prime\prime})^{!}f^{*}h^{*}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\textup{low}}-\delta_{\textup{upp}}\rangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(h^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime})^{*}g^{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartriangle}
\textstyle{(g^{\prime\prime})^{!}(h\circ f)^{*}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\textup{out}}\rangle}}

4.3.
Functoriality for cohomological correspondences

We tabulate some situations where cohomological correspondences can be pushed forward or pulled back.

4.3.1.
Pushforward functoriality for cohomological correspondences

Suppose we have a map of correspondences

(4.3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

Definition 4.3.1
.

The map of correspondences (
4.3.1
) is called
left pushable
if the square with vertices
(C,A_{0},D,B_{0})
is pushable in the sense of Definition
4.2.1
.

Assume (
4.3.1
) is left pushable. Then for any cohomological correspondence
c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cc}c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}
, there is a “pushforward correspondence”
f_{!}(\cc):d_{0}^{*}f_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow d_{1}^{!}f_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}
, defined as the composition

d_{0}^{*}f_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0}\xrightarrow{\triangledown}f_{!}c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cc}f_{!}c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}\rightarrow d_{1}^{!}f_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}

where the rightmost map is the natural base change transformation. Thus
\cc\mapsto f_{!}(\cc)
defines a linear map

(4.3.2)

f_{!}:\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{D}(f_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0},f_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}).

4.3.2.
Pullback functoriality for cohomological correspondences

Consider the diagram of correspondences in (
4.3.1
).

Definition 4.3.2
.

The diagram of correspondences (
4.3.1
) is called
right pullable
if the square with vertices
(C,A_{1},D,B_{1})
is pullable in the sense of Definition
4.2.1
.

In this case, we also say that the map of correspondences
f:C\to D
is right pullable, with
defect

\delta_{f}
defined to be the defect of the square
(C,A_{1},D,B_{1})
, i.e., the relative dimension of the quasi-smooth map
\widetilde{c}_{1}\colon C\to D\times_{A_{1}}B_{1}
.

Suppose (
4.3.1
) is right pullable. Then for any cohomological correspondence
d_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cc}d_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}
there is a “pullback correspondence”
f^{*}(\cc):c_{0}^{*}f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow c_{1}^{!}f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{f}\rangle}
defined as the composition

c_{0}^{*}f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}=f^{*}d_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cc}f^{*}d_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}\xrightarrow{\vartriangle}c_{1}^{!}f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{f}\rangle}.

Thus
\cc\mapsto f^{*}(\cc)
defines a linear map

(4.3.3)

f^{*}:\mathrm{Corr}_{D}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{f}\rangle}).

Remark 4.3.3
.

Similarly, we say the map of correspondences
f:C\to D
is
right topologically pullable
(with defect
\delta_{f}=0
) if the square with vertices
(C,A_{1},D,B_{1})
is topologically pullable in the sense of Remark
4.2.4
.
We can then similarly define a pullback operation

(4.3.4)

f^{*}:\mathrm{Corr}_{D}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1})

using Remark
4.2.6
.

4.4.
Base change for cohomological correspondences

In this subsection we formulate a base change result for cohomological correspondences (Theorem
4.4.2
), following
[
FYZ22
, §5]
.

4.4.1.
Setup

Suppose we are given a commutative diagram of derived Artin stacks

(4.4.1)

satisfying the following conditions:

(b)

\textstyle{U_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
