\scriptstyle{z_{1}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C_{W}}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}}
\textstyle{W_{1}}

Moreover, the right square
(U_{1},V_{1},S_{1},W_{1})
above has defect zero.

4.4.2.

We view
C_{S}
as a correspondence between
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
, and similarly for
C_{U},C_{V}
and
C_{W}
. Let
\mathcal{K}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S_{i}};\mathbf{Q})
for
i\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{S}}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})
.

4.4.3.
Push
\circ
pull

By assumption, the back face of (
4.4.1
) is pullable as a map of correspondences
\pi:C_{U}\to C_{S}
, so the map

(4.4.5)

\pi^{*}:\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{S}}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{U}}(\pi_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\pi}\rangle})

is defined (where the defect
\delta_{\pi}
is defined in Definition
4.3.1
). By assumption, the top face of (
4.4.1
) is pushable as a map of correspondences
f:C_{U}\to C_{V}
, so the map

(4.4.6)

f_{!}:\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{U}}(\pi^{*}_{0}\mathcal{K}_{0},\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\pi}\rangle})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{V}}(f_{0!}\pi_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},f_{1!}\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\pi}\rangle})

is defined. The composition of (
4.4.5
) and (
4.4.6
) applied to
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{S}}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})
gives an element

(4.4.7)

f_{!}\pi^{*}(\mathfrak{s})\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{V}}(f_{0!}\pi_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},f_{1!}\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\pi}\rangle}).

4.4.4.
Pull
\circ
push

Similarly, since the bottom face of the diagram (
4.4.1
) is left pushable and the front face is right pullable, the cohomological correspondence

(4.4.8)

g^{*}z_{!}(\mathfrak{s})\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{V}}(g_{0}^{*}z_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0},g_{1}^{*}z_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{g}\rangle})

is defined.

4.4.5.

We are now ready to formulate the base change theorem, expressing the compatibility of push
\circ
pull and pull
\circ
push.

By assumption, the square
(U_{0},V_{0},S_{0},W_{0})
in (
4.4.1
) is pushable, so we get a base change natural transformation

(4.4.9)

g^{*}_{0}z_{0!}\xrightarrow{\triangledown}f_{0!}\pi_{0}^{*}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S_{0}};\mathbf{Q})\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({V_{0}};\mathbf{Q}).

By assumption, the square
(U_{1},V_{1},S_{1},W_{1})
in (
4.4.1
) is pullable with defect zero, so we get a base change natural transformation

(4.4.10)

\pi_{1}^{*}z_{1}^{!}\xrightarrow{\vartriangle}f_{1}^{!}g^{*}_{1}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({W_{1}};\mathbf{Q})\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({U_{1}};\mathbf{Q}).

By adjunction (cf. Remark
4.2.5
), (
4.4.10
) gives a base change natural transformation

(4.4.11)

f_{1!}\pi_{1}^{*}\to g^{*}_{1}z_{1!}:\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S_{1}};\mathbf{Q})\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({V_{1}};\mathbf{Q}).

We have an equality of defects
\delta_{\pi}=\delta_{g}

[
FYZ22
, Lemma 5.1.1]
.

Example 4.4.1
.

Suppose
(U_{0},V_{0},S_{0},W_{0})
and
(U_{1},V_{1},S_{1},W_{1})
are derived Cartesian. In this case, the sources and targets of
f_{!}\pi^{*}(\mathfrak{s})
and
g^{*}z_{!}(\mathfrak{s})
are identified by the proper base change isomorphisms

(4.4.12)

f_{0!}\pi_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\xrightarrow{\diamond}g^{*}_{0}z_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{1!}\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\pi}\rangle}\xrightarrow{\diamond}g^{*}_{1}z_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{g}\rangle}.

Theorem 4.4.2

(Base change for cohomological correspondences)
.

Let the notation be as in §
4.4.1
.
Then for every
\mathcal{K}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S_{0}};\mathbf{Q})
,
\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({S_{1}};\mathbf{Q})
, and
\mathfrak{s}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{S}}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})
, the following diagram commutes:

(4.4.13)

\textstyle{g^{*}_{0}z_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}z_{!}(\mathfrak{s})}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{eq: bc cc 4})}
\textstyle{g^{*}_{1}z_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{g}\rangle}}
\textstyle{f_{0!}\pi_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}\pi^{*}(\mathfrak{s})}
\textstyle{f_{1!}\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-\delta_{\pi}\rangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{eq: match target})}

(Here we use
[
FYZ22
, Lemma 5.1.1]
to match the twists.)

In particular, when both
(U_{0},V_{0},S_{0},W_{0})
and
(U_{1},V_{1},S_{1},W_{1})
are derived Cartesian, we have an equality of cohomological correspondences on
C_{V}

(4.4.14)

f_{!}\pi^{*}(\mathfrak{s})=g^{*}z_{!}(\mathfrak{s})

under the isomorphisms (
4.4.12
).

Proof.

The proof of
[
FYZ22
, Theorem 5.1.3]
works verbatim.
∎

5.
The Lu–Zheng categorical trace

In this section we adapt the framework of Lu–Zheng
[
LZ22b
]
, which gave a new perspective on ULA sheaves and Lefschetz-Verdier formulas in the
\ell
-adic setting, to the derived category of motives. For a derived Artin stack
S
over a field, we define symmetric monoidal 2-categories
\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\LZ(S)^{*}
, in which objects are motivic sheaves on derived Artin stacks over
S
, morphisms are cohomological correspondences, and 2-morphisms are either pushforward or pullback of cohomological correspondences. Strongly universally locally acyclic motives are dualizable, so one can define the
categorical trace
of their endomorphisms. We use this to study relative Lefschetz-Verdier pairings and their behavior under pullback and pushforward.

Compared to
[
LZ22b
]
we introduce some technical enhancements, which would apply equally well to the
\ell
-adic setting and simplify some proofs in
[
FYZ22
]
.

•

We introduce an extended version of the Lu–Zheng category whose morphisms include higher Exts. This means that the trace of an endomorphism (of a dualizable object) can be valued in higher degree Chow groups, which is responsible for eventually promoting the sheaf-function correspondence to a sheaf-
cycle
correspondence.

•

We introduce a variant of the Lu–Zheng category adapted to pullbacks instead of pushforwards. This is eventually used to prove the compatibility of the sheaf-cycle correspondence with pullbacks. For this, it is essential to incorporate derived algebraic geometry (unlike the pushforward version, where all the content is already found in the classical truncation).

•

We work with derived Artin stacks (as opposed to schemes), a generality which is needed in applications.

5.1.
Motivic Lu–Zheng categories

Let
S
be a locally finite type derived Artin stack over a field. Following work of Lu–Zheng
[
LZ22b
]
, we will define two 2-categories, which we denote
\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\LZ(S)^{*}
.

5.1.1.
Objects and morphisms

Both
\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\LZ(S)^{*}
have the following objects and 1-morphisms:

•

The objects are pairs
(A,\mathcal{K}_{A})
where
A
is a locally finite type derived Artin stack over
S
, and
\mathcal{K}_{A}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
.

•

A
morphism
from
(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})
to
(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
is a triple
(c,i,\cc)
where
c=(A_{0}\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}A_{1})
is a correspondence,
i\in\mathbf{Z}
, and
\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
is a cohomological correspondence from
\mathcal{K}_{0}
to
\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle}
with respect to
c
.

In particular,
\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\LZ(S)^{*}
have the same objects and 1-morphisms.

Notation 5.1.1
.

We will generally use a lower-case Roman letter such as
c
or
d
, with no subscripts, as shorthand for a correspondence involving the corresponding upper-case Roman letter, such as
C
or
D
.

The composition of morphisms
(c,i,\cc)\colon(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})\rightarrow(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
and
(d,j,\dd)\colon(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})\rightarrow(A_{2},\mathcal{K}_{2})
is
(e,i+j,\ee)
, where
e
is the outer correspondence in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

where the diamond is (derived) Cartesian, and
\ee
is the composition

(d_{0}^{\prime})^{*}c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}\xrightarrow{\cc}(d_{0}^{\prime})^{*}c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle}\xrightarrow{\diamond}(c_{1}^{\prime})^{!}d_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle}\xrightarrow{\dd}(c_{1}^{\prime})^{!}d_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{2}{\scriptstyle\langle-i-j\rangle}

5.1.2.
2-morphisms

The 2-morphisms of
\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\LZ(S)^{*}
differ:

•

In
\LZ(S)_{!}
: Given two morphisms
(c,i,\cc)
and
(d,j,\dd)
from
(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})
to
(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
, a
2-morphism

(c,i,\cc)\rightarrow(d,j,\dd)
in
\LZ(S)_{!}
is a map of correspondences

(5.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

in which
f
is proper (hence the map of correspondences is left pushable by Example
4.2.3
), and such that
\dd=f_{!}\cc
in the sense of §
4.3.1
(so that
i=j
).

•

In
\LZ(S)^{*}
: Given two morphisms
(c,i,\cc)
and
(d,j,\dd)
from
(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})
to
(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
, a
2-morphism

(c,i,\cc)\rightarrow(d,j,\dd)
is a map of correspondences (
5.1.1
) in which
f
is quasi-smooth (hence the map of correspondences is right pullable by Example
4.2.3
), and such that
\cc=f^{*}\dd
(so that
i-j
equals the defect
\delta_{f}
).

The composition of 2-morphisms is given by the obvious construction.

Remark 5.1.2
.

The category
\LZ(S)_{!}
is a graded motivic version of the category
\mathcal{C}_{S}
from
[
LZ22b
, Construction 2.6]
. This category is adapted to the purpose of proving relative Lefschetz-Verdier formulas, which concern the compatibility of pushforwards with the Lefschetz-Verdier pairing (cf. §
5.4
). The category
\LZ(S)^{*}
(which to our knowledge has not been previously considered) is adapted to proving compatibility of pullbacks with the Lefschetz-Verdier pairing.

Example 5.1.3
.

Let
f\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
be a morphism of derived Artin stacks over
S
. Then for
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
, we have a 1-morphism in
\LZ(S)_{!}
or
\LZ(S)^{*}

f_{\natural}\colon(A,\mathcal{K})\rightarrow(A^{\prime},f_{!}\mathcal{K})

given by the correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

equipped with the cohomological correspondence
\mathcal{K}=\Id^{*}\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{unit}}f^{!}f_{!}\mathcal{K}
.

For
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A^{\prime}};\mathbf{Q})
, we have a 1-morphism in
\LZ(S)_{!}
or
\LZ(S)^{*}

f^{\natural}\colon(A^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\rightarrow(A,f^{*}\mathcal{K})

given by the correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

equipped with the tautological cohomological correspondence
f^{*}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\rightarrow\Id^{!}(f^{*}\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
.

Example 5.1.4
.

Let

(5.1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{\prime}}

be a commutative diagram of derived Artin stacks over
S
.

Suppose (
5.1.2
) is left pushable as a map of correspondences. Let
(c,i,\cc)\colon(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})\rightarrow(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
be a morphism in
\LZ(S)_{!}
. Then, unraveling the definitions, there is tautologically a unique 2-morphism in
\LZ(S)_{!}
fitting into a commutative diagram with the pushforward cohomological correspondence
f_{!}\cc
from §
4.3.1
:

(5.1.3)

\textstyle{(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{0\natural}}
\scriptstyle{(c,i,\cc)}
\textstyle{(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1\natural}}
\textstyle{(A_{0}^{\prime},f_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c^{\prime},i,f_{!}\cc)}
\textstyle{(A^{\prime}_{1},f_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Now suppose instead that (
5.1.2
) is right pullable as a map of correspondences. Let
(c^{\prime},i^{\prime},\cc^{\prime})\colon(A_{0}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\prime})\rightarrow(A_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\prime})
be a morphism in
\LZ(S)^{*}
. Then, unraveling the definitions, there is tautologically a unique 2-morphism in
\LZ(S)^{*}
fitting into a commutative diagram with the pullback cohomological correspondence
f^{*}\cc^{\prime}
from §
4.3.2
:

(5.1.4)

\textstyle{(A_{0},f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c,i,f^{*}\cc^{\prime})}
\textstyle{(A_{1},f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A_{0}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c^{\prime},i^{\prime},\cc^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}^{\natural}}
\textstyle{(A^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}^{\natural}}

where
i=i^{\prime}+\delta_{f}
.

5.1.3.
Symmetric monoidal structure

We construct a symmetric monoidal structure on
\LZ(S)^{*}
and
\LZ(S)_{!}
. In both cases, we define the tensor product of objects as

(A,\mathcal{K}_{A})\otimes(B,\mathcal{K}_{B})\coloneqq(A\times_{S}B,\mathcal{K}_{A}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{B})

where we recall that
\mathcal{K}_{A}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{K}_{B}\coloneqq\pr_{A}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes\pr_{B}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{B}
for the projection maps
A\xleftarrow{\pr_{A}}A\times_{S}B\xrightarrow{\pr_{B}}B
.

The tensor product of 1-morphisms
(c,i,\cc)\colon(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})\rightarrow(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
and
(d,j,\dd)\colon(B_{0},\mathcal{L}_{0})\rightarrow(B_{1},\mathcal{L}_{1})
is the product (over
S
) correspondence
c\times_{S}d
equipped with the cohomological correspondence

(c_{0}\times_{S}d_{0})^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{0}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{L}_{0})\cong c_{0}^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{0})\boxtimes_{S}d_{0}^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{0})\xrightarrow{\cc\boxtimes_{S}\dd}c_{1}^{!}(\mathcal{K}_{1}){\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle}\boxtimes_{S}d_{1}^{!}(\mathcal{L}_{1}){\scriptstyle\langle-j\rangle}\rightarrow(c_{1}\times_{S}d_{1})^{!}(\mathcal{K}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{L}_{1}){\scriptstyle\langle-i-j\rangle}

where the last map is adjoint to the Künneth formula

(c_{1}\times_{S}d_{1})_{!}(\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\prime}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime})\cong c_{1!}\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\prime}\boxtimes_{S}d_{1!}\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime}

from Lemma
3.8.2
.

In both
\LZ(S)^{*}
and
\LZ(S)_{!}
, the tensor product of 2-morphisms is induced by product of morphisms of stacks over
S
.

Example 5.1.5
.

The monoidal unit in both
(\LZ(S)_{!},\otimes)
and
(\LZ(S)^{*},\otimes)
is the object
(S,\mathbf{Q}_{S})
.

5.2.
Dualizable objects

Any symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\otimes)
has a notion of
dualizable object
: this means an object
c\in\mathsf{C}
such that there exists a
dual

c^{\vee}\in\mathsf{C}
and evaluation (resp. coevaluation) morphisms
\ev_{c}\colon c^{\vee}\otimes c\rightarrow 1_{\mathsf{C}}
(resp.
\coev_{c}\colon 1_{\mathsf{C}}\rightarrow c\otimes c^{\vee}
) such that the composites

c\xrightarrow{\coev_{c}\otimes\Id_{c}}c\otimes c^{\vee}\otimes c\xrightarrow{\Id_{c}\otimes\ev_{c}}c,\hskip 28.45274ptc^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\Id_{c^{\vee}}\otimes\coev_{c}}c^{\vee}\otimes c\otimes c^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\ev_{c}\otimes\Id_{c^{\vee}}}c^{\vee}

are isomorphic to the respective identity morphisms.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

Let
(A,\mathcal{K})
be a dualizable object of
\LZ(S)_{!}
or
\LZ(S)^{*}
. Then the dual of
(A,\mathcal{K})
is
(A,\mathbf{D}_{A/S}\mathcal{K})
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
LZ22b
, Proposition 2.11]
works verbatim.
∎

Corollary 5.2.2
.

Let
(A,\mathcal{K})
be a dualizable object of
\LZ(S)_{!}
or
\LZ(S)^{*}
. Then the canonical map
\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathbf{D}_{A/S}(\mathbf{D}_{A/S}\mathcal{K})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

In any symmetric monoidal category, any dualizable object is isomorphic to its double dual.
∎

Proposition 5.2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
be USLA over
S
. Then
(A,\mathcal{K})
is dualizable in
\LZ(S)_{!}
or
\LZ(S)^{*}
.

Proof.

We will show that
(A,\mathbf{D}_{A/S}(\mathcal{K}))
is dual to
(A,\mathcal{K})
by explicitly constructing the evaluation and coevaluation morphisms (satisfying the necessary properties). In fact, the construction of the evaluation morphism does not invoke the USLA hypothesis: it is given by the correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

equipped with the tautological cohomological correspondence

\Delta^{*}(\mathcal{K}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbf{D}_{A/S}\mathcal{K})\cong\mathcal{K}\otimes_{S}\mathbf{D}_{A/S}(\mathcal{K})\rightarrow\mathbf{D}_{A/S}.

The coevaluation morphism will have underlying correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{S}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Using Proposition
3.8.5
, we have the following isomorphisms in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{!}(\mathcal{K}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbf{D}_{A/S}\mathcal{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{!}\mathcal{H}om_{A\times_{S}A}(\pr_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K},\pr_{0}^{!}\mathcal{K})\cong\mathcal{H}om_{A}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
Prop
3.8.5

Thus
\Id_{\mathcal{K}}\in\Hom_{A}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K})
induces a map
\mathbf{Q}_{A}\rightarrow\Delta^{!}(\mathcal{K}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbf{D}_{A/S}\mathcal{K})
, which defines the coevaluation morphism.

The composite
(\Id_{c}\otimes\ev_{c})\circ(\coev_{c}\otimes\Id_{c})
is supported on the outer correspondence in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{S}A\times_{S}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{01}\times\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id\times\pr_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

and unraveling the definitions shows that the resulting cohomological correspondence is isomorphic to the identity morphism. A similar analysis applies to
(\ev_{c}\otimes\Id_{c^{\vee}})\circ(\Id_{c^{\vee}}\otimes\coev_{c})
.
∎

5.3.
Categorical traces

Recall that any endomorphism
t\in\End(c)
of a dualizable object
c
in any symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\otimes)
with unit
\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}}
has a notion of
trace

\Tr(t)\in\End(\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}})
, defined as the composite

\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}}\xrightarrow{\coev_{c}}c\otimes c^{\vee}\xrightarrow{t\otimes\Id}c\otimes c^{\vee}\cong c^{\vee}\otimes c\xrightarrow{\ev_{c}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}}.

If
\mathsf{C}
is a 2-category, then
\End(\mathbf{1}_{\mathsf{C}})
forms a 1-category, denoted
\Omega\mathsf{C}
.

Specializing this construction, let
S
be a derived Artin stack locally of finite type over a field.
We obtain two categories
\Omega\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\Omega\LZ(S)^{*}
with the same objects: in both cases the objects are triples
(F,i,\alpha)
where
F
is a derived Artin stack locally of finite type over
S
(identified with the correspondence
S\leftarrow F\rightarrow S
),
i\in\mathbf{Z}
, and
\alpha\in\mathrm{Corr}_{F}(\mathbf{Q}_{S},\mathbf{Q}_{S}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})=\CH_{i}(F/S)
is a relative Chow cycle over
S
of degree
i
.

The morphisms in
\Omega\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\Omega\LZ(S)^{*}
differ:

•

In
\Omega\LZ(S)_{!}
, morphisms
(F,i,\alpha)\rightarrow(F^{\prime},j,\beta)
are proper maps
f\colon F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
over
S
such that
f_{*}\alpha=\beta\in\CH_{j}(F^{\prime}/S)
(so that
i=j
).

•

In
\Omega\LZ(S)^{*}
, morphisms
(F,i,\alpha)\rightarrow(F^{\prime},j,\beta)
are quasi-smooth maps
f\colon F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
over
S
such that
f^{!}\beta=\alpha\in\CH_{i}(F/S)
(so that
i-j=d(f)
is the relative dimension of
f
).

Given a dualizable object
(A,\mathcal{K})\in\LZ(S)_{!}
or
\LZ(S)^{*}
, an endomorphism of
(A,\mathcal{K})
in
\LZ(S)_{!}
or
\LZ(S)^{*}
consists of a correspondence
(A\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}A)
, an integer
i\in\mathbf{Z}
, and a cohomological correspondence
\cc\in\Hom_{C}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
. The
trace
of such an endomorphism is the triple
(\Fix(C),i,\alpha\in\CH_{i}(\Fix(C)/S)
). We will refer to
\alpha
as the
trace of
\cc
, and write

(5.3.1)

\Tr_{C}(\cc)\coloneqq\alpha\in\CH_{i}(\Fix(C)/S).

In other words, for every correspondence
(A\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}A)
of derived Artin stacks over
S
and every
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
, we obtain a canonical linear map

(5.3.2)

\Tr_{C}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})\to\CH_{i}(\Fix(C)/S).

In particular, if
S=\Spec(\mathbf{F})
, we obtain a trace map valued in cycle classes on
\Fix(C)
.

5.4.
Lefschetz-Verdier pairings

The general theory of pairings in symmetric monoidal 2-categories is documented in
[
LZ22b
, §1]
. In particular, given a symmetric monoidal 2-category
(\mathsf{C},\otimes)
and morphisms
u\colon c\rightarrow d
and
v\colon d\rightarrow c
in
\mathsf{C}
, with
c
dualizable, we have the pairing

(5.4.1)

\langle u,v\rangle\coloneqq\Tr(v\circ u)\in\Omega\mathsf{C}.

Example 5.4.1
.

If
d=c
and
v=\Id_{c}
, we have

\langle u,\Id_{c}\rangle=\Tr(u)\in\Omega\mathsf{C}.

Suppose we have a diagram in
\mathsf{C}

(5.4.2)

\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{c^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\textstyle{d^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\alpha}
\textstyle{c^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\beta}

with
c
and
c^{\prime}
dualizable. Then by
[
LZ22b
, Construction 1.8]
we have a morphism

(5.4.3)

\langle u,v\rangle\to\langle u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\rangle\in\Omega\mathsf{C}.

5.4.1.
Compatibility with pushforward

Let

(5.4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}_{0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

be a commutative diagram of derived Artin stacks over

. Write

(resp.

for the composite correspondence of

and

(resp.
C^{\prime}
and

) and

.

Theorem 5.4.2
.

Assume in (

) that

and
f_{0!}\mathcal{K}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({A_{0}};\mathbf{Q})

.
8
8

8

This second assumption is automatic if

, which will be satisfied in all the situations where we will apply Theorem

.
Let

and

Then
\Fix(h)\colon\Fix(E)\rightarrow\Fix(E^{\prime})
is proper and
9
9

9

In forming this trace, we are implicitly using that

is geometric by Remark

(since

is assumed to be representable).

\Fix(h)_{*}\langle u,v\rangle=\langle f_{!}u,g_{!}v\rangle\in\CH_{i+j}(\Fix(E^{\prime})).

The analogous result for schemes and

, and the argument is the same in essence. From the assumed left pushability of

regarded as a map of correspondences, applying (

) gives a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

5.4.2.
Compatibility with pullback

Consider the same diagram (

) and again write

(resp.
E^{\prime})
for the composite correspondence of
C
and
D
(resp.

and

) and

.

Theorem 5.4.3
.

Assume in (

Then

is quasi-smooth and

In forming this trace, we are implicitly using that

.

\Fix(h)^{!}\langle u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\rangle=\langle f^{*}u^{\prime},g^{*}v^{\prime}\rangle\in\CH_{i+j+\delta_{h}}(\Fix(E)).

6.
Motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence

In this section we develop the motivic sheaf-cycle correspondence, which is based on the trace map constructed in §
5.3
.
This material is a motivic lift of
[
FYZ22
, §4]
, much of which carries over verbatim (in those cases we omit the proofs). The only aspect that is substantially different is Proposition
6.2.2
, which we prove using the motivic Lu–Zheng categorical trace.

6.1.
The trace

Let

(6.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

be a correspondence of derived Artin stacks over a field
\mathbf{F}
.
For every
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
and
i\in\mathbf{Z}
, the construction of §
5.3
gives a trace map

(6.1.2)

\Tr_{C}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})\to\CH_{i}(\Fix(C)).

Indeed, since
\mathcal{K}
is geometric (by assumption) and USLA over
\Spec(k)
(Example
3.7.3
), Proposition
5.2.3
implies that
(A,\mathcal{K})
is dualizable in
\LZ(\mathbf{F})^{*}
or
\LZ(\mathbf{F})_{!}
.

6.2.
Functoriality of the trace

We study the compatibility of pushforward and pullback on cohomological correspondences and Chow groups (see §
4.3
) under formation of traces.
We consider a map of correspondences of derived Artin stacks over a field
\mathbf{F}
:

(6.2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}=f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

6.2.1.
Proper pushforward

The following result is a motivic lift of
[
FYZ22
, Proposition 4.5.1]
.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

With notation as in (
6.2.1
), assume that
f_{0},f
are proper and let
\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
where
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
. Then
\Fix(f)\colon\Fix(C)\rightarrow\Fix(D)
is proper and we have

\Tr_{D}(f_{!}\cc)=\Fix(f)_{*}(\Tr_{C}(\cc))\in\CH_{i}(\Fix(D)).

Proof.

Apply Theorem
5.4.2
with
S\coloneqq\Spec\mathbf{F}
,
A_{0}=A_{1}\coloneqq A
,
\mathcal{K}_{0}=\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{K}
,
u\coloneqq\cc
, and
v\coloneqq\Id
.
∎

6.2.2.
Quasi-smooth pullback

The following result is a motivic lift of
[
FYZ22
, Proposition 4.5.4]
.

Proposition 6.2.2
.

With notation as in (
6.2.1
), assume that
f_{1}
is smooth and
f
is quasi-smooth. Let
\delta=d(f)-d(f_{1})
. Let
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{D}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
where
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({B};\mathbf{Q})
. Then
\Fix(f)\colon\Fix(C)\rightarrow\Fix(D)
is quasi-smooth and we have

\Tr_{C}(f^{*}\mathfrak{d})=\Fix(f)^{!}(\Tr_{D}(\mathfrak{d}))\in\CH_{i+\delta}(\Fix(C)).

Proof.

Apply Theorem
5.4.3
with
S\coloneqq\Spec\mathbf{F}
,
A_{0}=A_{1}\coloneqq A
,
\mathcal{K}_{0}=\mathcal{K}_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{K}
,
u\coloneqq\dd
, and
v\coloneqq\Id
.
∎

6.3.
The fundamental class as a trace

Let
A\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}A
be a correspondence of derived Artin stacks over
\mathbf{F}
.
If
c_{1}
is quasi-smooth and
A
is smooth over
\mathbf{F}
, then
\Fix(C)
is quasi-smooth over
\mathbf{F}
of relative dimension
d(c_{1})
, so there is a fundamental class (§
3.6
)

(6.3.1)

[\Fix(C)]\in\CH_{d(c_{1})}(\Fix(C)).

On the other hand, regarding the relative fundamental class
[c_{1}]\in\CH_{d(c_{1})}(C/A)
as a map
c_{1}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{A}\rightarrow c_{1}^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{A}{\scriptstyle\langle-d(c_{1})\rangle}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({C};\mathbf{Q})
, the composite

c_{0}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{A}\cong\mathbf{Q}_{A}\cong c_{1}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{A}\xrightarrow{[c_{1}]}c_{1}^{!}\mathbf{Q}_{A}{\scriptstyle\langle-d(c_{1})\rangle}

defines a cohomological correspondence
\cc_{A}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathbf{Q}_{A},\mathbf{Q}_{A}{\scriptstyle\langle-d(c_{1})\rangle})
.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

If
c_{1}
is quasi-smooth and
A
is smooth, then we have

[\Fix(C)]=\Tr_{C}(\cc_{A})\in\CH_{d(c_{1})}(\Fix(C)).

Proof.

Consider the map of correspondences

(6.3.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pt}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

where
\pt=\Spec(\mathbf{F})
.
By assumption
\pi_{1}
is smooth and
c_{1}
is quasi-smooth, so
\pi
is quasi-smooth and (
6.3.2
) is right pullable.
Unravelling definitions, we can write
\cc_{A}
as the pullback of the trivial cohomological correspondence
\cc_{\pt}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\pt}(\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q})
(cf.
[
FYZ22
, Lemma 9.4.2]
):

\pi^{*}\cc_{\pt}=\cc_{A}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathbf{Q}_{A},\mathbf{Q}_{A}{\scriptstyle\langle-d(c_{1})\rangle}).

By Proposition
6.2.2
we have

\Tr_{C}(\pi^{*}\cc_{\pt})=\Fix(\pi)^{!}(\Tr_{\pt}(\cc_{\pt}))=\Fix(\pi)^{!}[\pt]=[\Fix(C)]\in\CH_{d(c_{1})}(\Fix(C)),

whence the claim.
∎

6.4.
Frobenius-twisted trace

6.4.1.
Frobenius

We take
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{q}
to be a finite field.Any derived Artin stack over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
is equipped with a Frobenius endomorphism
\Frob
, which in terms of the functor of points is the absolute Frobenius
\Frob_{q}
on the test scheme.
That is, for any derived Artin stack
X
over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
, we denote by
\Frob:X\to X
the morphism sending an
R
-point
x:\Spec(R)\to X
to the composite

\Spec(R)\xrightarrow{\Frob_{q}}\Spec(R)\xrightarrow{x}X

for every commutative
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-algebra
R
.

6.4.2.
Fix vs. Sht

For a correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
, we will let
\Sht(C)
(or sometimes
\Sht_{A}
) be the derived fibered product

(6.4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sht(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c_{0},c_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Id,\Frob)}

This derived fibered product can be also be presented by the derived Cartesian square

(6.4.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sht(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Frob\circ c_{0},c_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

which is the “fixed point Cartesian square” for the correspondence

(6.4.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Frob\circ c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

where
C^{(1)}\coloneqq C
but with the left map twisted by
\Frob
. In other words, we have a canonical identification

(6.4.4)

\Sht(C)=\Fix(C^{(1)}).

6.4.3.
Sht-valued trace

Given
\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
and a cohomological correspondence
\cc\colon c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}
on
C
, plus the canonical Weil structure
\Frob^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\cong\mathcal{K}_{0}
(because
A
is defined over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
), we have a cohomological correspondence
\cc^{(1)}\colon(\Frob\circ c_{0})^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\rightarrow c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}
. In this way we obtain a linear isomorphism

\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Corr}_{C^{(1)}}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})

sending
\cc\mapsto\cc^{(1)}
. If
\mathcal{K}_{0}
is geometric and
\mathcal{K}_{1}=\mathcal{K}_{0}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle}
, then we define

\Tr_{C}^{\Sht}(\cc)\coloneqq\Tr_{C}(\cc^{(1)})\in\CH_{i}(\Fix(C^{(1)}))=\CH_{i}(\Sht(C)).

This determines a linear map

(6.4.5)

\Tr_{C}^{\Sht}:\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0}\langle{-i}\rangle)\to\CH_{i}(\Sht(C)).

6.4.4.
The fundamental class of
\Sht(C)

In the situation of §
6.4.2
, Proposition
6.3.1
yields:

Corollary 6.4.1
.

If
c_{1}
is quasi-smooth and
A
is smooth over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
, then we have

\Tr_{C}^{\Sht}(\cc_{A})=[\Sht(C)]\in\CH_{d(c_{1})}(\Sht(C))

where
\mathbf{Q}_{A}
is equipped with its natural Weil structure.

Remark 6.4.2
.

It is interesting to ask in what generality Corollary
6.4.1
holds without the smoothness of
A
.
Indeed, it is shown in
[
FYZ22
, Lemma 4.2.1]
that
\Sht(C)
is quasi-smooth (and hence admits a fundamental class) as long as
c_{1}
is quasi-smooth.

6.5.
Shift and twist

6.5.1.

Let
A\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}A
be a correspondence over a field
\Spec\mathbf{F}
. Given
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({A};\mathbf{Q})
and
\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})
, the map
\cc:c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}\to c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}
induces for every
m,n\in\mathbf{Z}
a map

c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0}[m](n)\to c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1}[m](n)

which we denote by
\mathbb{T}_{[m](n)}\cc
.
The assignment
\cc\mapsto\mathbb{T}_{[m](n)}\cc
defines an isomorphism

\mathbb{T}_{[m](n)}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0}[m](n),\mathcal{K}_{1}[m](n)).

6.5.2.

The trace map
\Tr_{C}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})\rightarrow\CH_{i}(\Fix(C))
satisfies the identity

\Tr_{C}(\mathbb{T}_{[m](n)}\cc)=(-1)^{m}\cdot\Tr_{C}(\cc)\in\CH_{i}(\Fix(C)).

6.5.3.
Sht-valued trace

Suppose
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{q}
and consider the map
\Tr^{\Sht}_{C}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})\rightarrow\CH_{i}(\Sht(C))
(§
6.4.3
).
We have the identity

(6.5.1)

\Tr_{C}^{\Sht}(\mathbb{T}_{[m](n)}\cc)=(-1)^{m}q^{-n}\cdot\Tr_{C}^{\Sht}(\cc)\in\CH_{i}(\Sht(C)).

7.
Specialization and motivic local terms

The main result of this section is Theorem
7.5.1
, which says that for a correspondence
c=(Y\leftarrow C\rightarrow Y)
of derived Artin stacks over a field
\mathbf{F}
, the trace of a cohomological correspondence supported on
c
can be calculated after restriction to a closed substack
Z\hookrightarrow Y
, provided that
c
is “contracting near
Z
”. We refer to Definition
7.2.1
for the meaning of the latter condition; for now we just mention that it is a condition on classical truncations. In fact, the entirety of this section deals only with properties and constructions of underlying classical truncations. Hence
for this section alone, we change our default conventions so that all constructions (fibered products, etc.) occur within classical algebraic geometry
.

The results in this section have previously appeared for schemes and
\ell
-adic coefficients in
[
Var07
]
, and then for schemes and motivic coefficients in
[
Jin24
]
. Our only contribution is of a technical nature: we generalize their arguments to (higher) Artin stacks and motivic coefficients. This is needed in applications, in the present paper as well as in in other work-in-progress.

7.1.
Ayoub’s nearby cycles functor

We work over a field
\mathbf{F}
. Let
i\colon s\hookrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
be the origin and
j\colon\eta\hookrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
its complement. Suppose we have a morphism of Artin stacks
f\colon Y\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
. For
?\in\{s,\eta\}
the subscript
?
will denote base change to
?
. Thus we have a commutative diagram

(7.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where the squares are Cartesian.

Ayoub constructed and analyzed the
motivic nearby cycles functor
on schemes, in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
,
Ayo14
]
. In
[
HL22
, §A.2]
, Ayoub’s construction of the
tame nearby cycles functor
(part of the total motivic nearby cycles) is extended to Artin stacks for
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({-};\mathbf{Q})
, essentially by repeating Ayoub’s construction verbatim. We denote this functor

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{\eta}};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{s}};\mathbf{Q}).

It satisfies the following properties:

(a)

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}
is lax-monoidal, so in particular there are binatural transformations

(7.1.2)

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\rightarrow\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{s}};\mathbf{Q})

for all
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{\eta}};\mathbf{Q})
. If
Y_{0},Y_{1}
are Artin stacks over
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
, then as a special case of (
7.1.2
) we have natural maps
11
11

11

If we worked with the total nearby cycles functor instead of the tame part, then these maps would be isomorphisms.

(7.1.3)

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{0}}(\mathcal{K}_{0})\boxtimes_{s}\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{1}}(\mathcal{K}_{1})\rightarrow\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{0}\times_{\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}}Y_{1}}(\mathcal{K}_{0}\boxtimes_{\eta}\mathcal{K}_{1})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{0s}\times_{s}Y_{1s}};\mathbf{Q})

for all
\mathcal{K}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{i}};\mathbf{Q})
.

(b)

For any morphism
g\colon Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
, there are natural transformations

(7.1.4)

g_{s}^{*}\circ\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}\rightarrow\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y^{\prime}}\circ g_{\eta}^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{\eta}};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y^{\prime}_{s}};\mathbf{Q})

and

(7.1.5)

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y^{\prime}}\circ g_{\eta}^{!}\rightarrow g_{s}^{!}\circ\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{\eta}};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y^{\prime}_{s}};\mathbf{Q}),

which are both isomorphisms if
g
is smooth.

(c)

The are natural transformations

(7.1.6)

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}\circ g_{\eta*}\rightarrow g_{s*}\circ\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y^{\prime}_{\eta}};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{s}};\mathbf{Q})

and

(7.1.7)

g_{s!}\circ\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y^{\prime}}\rightarrow\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}\circ g_{u!}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y^{\prime}_{\eta}};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{s}};\mathbf{Q}),

which are both isomorphisms if
g
is proper.

(d)

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}
commutes with shifts and Tate twists.

Lemma 7.1.1
.

The functor
\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}
preserves geometricity (in the sense of §
3.3
).

Proof.

By (
7.1.4
) and the definition of geometricity, the statement can be checked after base change to a smooth atlas
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
where
Y^{\prime}
is a derived scheme.
Then the claim is
[
Jin24
, Lemma 6.1.9(2)]
(see also
[
Ayo07b
, Theorem 3.5.14]
for the case where
\mathbf{F}
is of characteristic zero).
∎

7.2.
Contracting correspondences

Let
c=(Y\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Y)
be a correspondence of locally noetherian (classical) Artin stacks. The following definitions generalize those of Varshavsky in
[
Var07
, Definition 1.5.1 and Definition 2.1.1]
.

Definition 7.2.1

(Contracting correspondences)
.

Let
Z\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed embedding defined by an ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Y}
.

(a)

We say that
Z
is
c
-invariant
if
c_{1}^{-1}(Z)
is set-theoretically contained in
c_{0}^{-1}(Z)
.

(b)

We say that
c

stabilizes

Z
if
c_{0}^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{Z})\subset c_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{Z})
(i.e.,
c_{1}^{-1}(Z)
is scheme-theoretically contained in
c_{0}^{-1}(Z)
).

(c)

We say that
c
is
contracting near

Z
if
c
stabilizes
Z
and there exists
n\in\N
such that
c_{0}^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{Z})^{n}\subset c_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{Z})^{n+1}
.

Note that (c)
\implies
(b)
\implies
(a).

Example 7.2.2

(Frobenius contracts)
.

Suppose
Y
is defined over a finite field
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{q}
and
c=(c_{0},c_{1})\colon C\rightarrow Y\times Y
is a correspondence stabilizing
Z\hookrightarrow Y
. Then the Frobenius-twisted correspondence
c^{(1)}\coloneqq(\Frob\circ c_{0},c_{1})\colon C\rightarrow Y\times Y
(§
6.4.2
) is contracting near
Z
.
Indeed, working locally on
Y
we may assume
Y
is noetherian, in which case this is proven in
[
Var07
, Lemma 2.2.3]
.

Construction 7.2.3

(Restricting correspondences to stable substacks)
.

For any closed substack
Z\hookrightarrow Y
such that
c
stabilizes
Z
, define the base-changed correspondence
c_{Z}
by the upper row in the commutative diagram

(7.2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

where the right square is Cartesian. (The hypothesis that
c
stabilizes
Z
is used to ensure that the pullback
C_{Z}\hookrightarrow C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}Y
factors over
Z
.)

Lemma 7.2.4
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

be a commutative diagram of locally noetherian Artin stacks. Then the induced map of normal cones
N_{Z^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})\rightarrow N_{Z}(Y)
has set-theoretic image in the zero-section
Z\subset N_{Z}(Y)
if and only if there exists
n
such that
f^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{Z}^{n})\subset\mathcal{I}_{Z^{\prime}}^{n+1}
.

Proof.

The same proof as in
[
Var07
, Lemma 1.4.3(b)]
works verbatim.
∎

7.3.
Specialization

Next we discuss specialization of Chow groups and cohomological correspondences in families.

7.3.1.
Specialization on Chow groups

For a commutative diagram (
7.1.1
) in which all squares are Cartesian, there is constructed in
[
DJK21
, §4.5.6]
12
12

12

Strictly speaking,
[
DJK21
]
operated in the schematic context, but
[
Kha19b
]
generalizes all the ingredients of the construction to derived Artin stacks.

a specialization map on relative Chow groups,

(7.3.1)

\mathfrak{sp}_{Y}\colon\CH_{*}(Y_{\eta}/\eta)\rightarrow\CH_{*}(Y_{s}/s).

7.3.2.
Specialization on cohomological correspondences

Suppose we have a correspondence of Artin stacks
Y_{0}\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Y_{1}
over
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
and a cohomological correspondence
\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{\eta}}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})
supported on
C_{\eta}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y_{i\eta}};\mathbf{Q})
.
13
13

13

We emphasize again that the subscripts
?\in\{\eta,s\}
indicate base change to
?
.

Then we have a cohomological correspondence

(7.3.2)

\Psi_{C}^{\mathrm{t}}(\cc)\colon c_{0s}^{*}\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{0}}(\mathcal{K}_{0})\rightarrow c_{1s}^{!}\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{1}}(\mathcal{K}_{1})

defined as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0s}^{*}\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{0}}(\mathcal{K}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{C}(c_{0\eta}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{C}(c_{1\eta}^{!}\mathcal{K}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1s}^{!}(\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{1}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{eq: pull Psi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{C}(\cc)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{eq: Psi shriek pull})}

The assignment
\cc\mapsto\Psi_{C}^{\mathrm{t}}(\cc)
defines a map

(7.3.3)

\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{\eta}}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{s}}(\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{0}}(\mathcal{K}_{0}),\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{1}}(\mathcal{K}_{1})).

7.3.3.
Specialization vs. trace

Under favorable conditions, the specialization of cohomological correspondences is compatible with formation of trace. This was shown by Varshavsky for
\ell
-adic sheaves on schemes in
[
Var07
, Proposition 1.3.5]
, and Jin adapted the argument to motivic sheaves on schemes in
[
Jin24
, Lemma 6.2.4]
. We record the statement in our more general context.

Proposition 7.3.1
.

Let
Y\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Y
be a correspondence of derived Artin stacks over
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
.
If
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({Y_{\eta}};\mathbf{Q})
is USLA over
\eta
, then the following diagram commutes:
14
14

14

To form the trace, we are implicitly using that
\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{0}}\mathcal{K}
is dualizable in
\LZ(S)_{!}
and
\LZ(S)^{*}
. This is because
\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y_{0}}\mathcal{K}
is USLA, by Example
3.7.3
, and geometric, by Lemma
7.1.1
, hence dualizable by Proposition
5.2.3
.

(7.3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{\eta}}(\mathcal{K}_{,}\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{s}}(\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}\mathcal{K},\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{Y}\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CH_{i}(\Fix(C_{\eta})/\eta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CH_{i}(\Fix(C_{s})/s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{C}^{\mathrm{t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr_{C_{\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr_{C_{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{sp}_{C}}

Proof.

By the same formal diagram chase as in the proof of
[
Jin24
, Lemma 6.2.4]
, this is reduced to the lax-monoidality of
\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}
.
∎

7.4.
Specialization to the normal cone

Let
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed immersion of Artin stacks over
\mathbf{F}
. Then there is a
deformation to the normal cone

D_{Z}(Y)
, which is a family of Artin stacks over
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
which restricts to the constant family
Y\times\mathbf{G}_{m}
over
\eta=\mathbf{G}_{m}
and the normal cone
N_{Z}(Y)
over
s
.
It may be constructed by forming the blow-up of
Y\times\mathbf{A}^{1}
along
Z\times\{0\}
and taking out the blow-up of
Y\times\{0\}
along
Z\times\{0\}
.
(We emphasize that we are considering the
classical
deformation to the normal cone rather than the derived version.)

The construction of
D_{Z}(Y)
is functorial in
Z
and
Y
. Following Varshavsky
[
Var07
]
, we use the notation
\widetilde{(-)}
for constructions induced by deformation to the normal cone, and
\widetilde{(-)}_{\eta}
or
\widetilde{(-)}_{s}
for the base changes to
\eta
or
s
, respectively. For example, given a commutative diagram

(7.4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

we get a map
\widetilde{g}\colon D_{Z^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})\rightarrow D_{Z}(Y)
.

7.4.1.
Specialization of cycle classes

Applying the specialization construction of §
7.3.1
to
D_{Z}(Y)
, we get a map

(7.4.2)

\mathfrak{sp}_{D_{Z}(Y)}\colon\CH_{*}(Y\times\eta/\eta)\to\CH_{*}(N_{Z}(Y)).

Composing with the pullback map
\CH_{*}(Y)\to\CH_{*}(Y\times\eta/\eta)
, we get a map
15
15

15

This can be defined more directly as in
[
DJK21
, Def. 3.2.4]
or
[
Kha19b
, Constr. 3.1]
, but this alternative description will be convenient for us.

(7.4.3)

\mathfrak{sp}_{Y,Z}\colon\CH_{*}(Y)\to\CH_{*}(N_{Z}(Y)).

7.4.2.
Specialization of sheaves

Formation of nearby cycles with respect to
D_{Z}(Y)
gives rise to a functor of specialization to the normal cone,

(7.4.4)

\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({N_{Z}(Y)};\mathbf{Q})

defined by the formula

(7.4.5)

\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}(\mathcal{K})\coloneqq\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{D_{Z}(Y)}(\pr^{*}\mathcal{K})

where
\pr\colon Y\times\mathbf{G}_{m}\rightarrow Y
is the projection to the first factor.

Given a commutative square (
7.4.1
), (
7.1.4
) gives a natural transformation

(7.4.6)

\widetilde{g}^{*}_{s}\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{sp}_{Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime}}g^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({N_{Z^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})};\mathbf{Q}).

Example 7.4.1
.

If
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow Y
is an isomorphism, then
D_{Z}(Y)
is the constant family
Z\times\mathbf{A}^{1}
, so
\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}=\Id
in that case. Taking
Z^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y^{\prime}
to be the identity map
Z=Z
in (
7.4.1
), we get a closed embedding
\widetilde{\iota}\colon Z\times\mathbf{A}^{1}\hookrightarrow D_{Z}(Y)
, which restricts to the constant embedding
Z\times\mathbf{G}_{m}\hookrightarrow Y\times\mathbf{G}_{m}
over
\eta
and the zero section
Z\hookrightarrow N_{Z}(Y)
over
s
. Hence in this case, (
7.4.6
) gives a map

(7.4.7)

\widetilde{\iota}_{s}^{*}\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}(\mathcal{K})\rightarrow\iota^{*}\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Z};\mathbf{Q}).

Proposition 7.4.2

(Verdier, Jin)
.

For all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})
, the map (
7.4.7
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The statement can be checked smooth locally on
Y
, so it reduces to the case where
Y
(and hence
Z
) is a scheme. Then it is
[
Jin24
, Proposition 6.3.14]
.
16
16

16

For schemes and
\ell
-adic sheaves, the analogous statement appears in work of Verdier
[
Ver83
, §8]
with a sketch of proof. A full proof is given by Varshavsky in
[
Var07
, §3]
.

∎

7.4.3.
Specialization of cohomological correspondences

Let
c=(Y\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Y)
be a correspondence of Artin stacks locally of finite type over a field
\mathbf{F}
. Suppose
c
stabilizes a closed substack
Z\hookrightarrow Y
. Then
c
can be restricted to a correspondence
(Z\leftarrow C_{Z}\rightarrow Z)
by Construction
7.2.3
. We consider the correspondence
\widetilde{c}

(7.4.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{Z}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{C_{Z}}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{Z}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{c}_{0}}

over
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{F}
, defined by deformation to the normal cone with respect to the vertical closed embeddings in (
7.2.1
).
Its fibers away from the origin are isomorphic to
c
and over
s:\Spec(\mathbf{F})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{1}_{\mathbf{F}}
it degenerates to the correspondence of normal cones:

(7.4.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{Z}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{C_{Z}}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{Z}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(c_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(c_{0})}

Given
\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})
, consider the specialization map on cohomological correspondences

(7.4.10)

\mathrm{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}_{N_{C_{Z}}(C)}(\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}(\mathcal{K}_{0}),\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}(\mathcal{K}_{1}))

defined as the composite of the pullback
\pr^{*}\colon\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K}_{0},\mathcal{K}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C\times\mathbf{G}_{m}}(\pr^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},\pr^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1})
and the map
\Psi^{\mathrm{t}}_{D_{C_{Z}}(C)}
(
7.3.3
).

7.4.4.
Specialization vs. trace

Applied to the deformation to the normal cone, Proposition
7.3.1
yields the following compatibility between the specialization maps of §
7.4.1
and §
7.4.3
.

Corollary 7.4.3
.

Let
c=(Y\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Y)
be a correspondence of Artin stacks locally of finite type over a field
\mathbf{F}
and let
Z\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed substack stabilized by
c
. Then for every
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})
, the following diagram commutes:

(7.4.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{N_{C_{Z}}(C)}(\mathrm{sp}_{C_{Z},C}\mathcal{K},\mathrm{sp}_{C_{Z},C}\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CH_{i}(\Fix(C))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CH_{i}(\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tr_{N_{C_{Z}}(C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}}

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{K}
is USLA over
\Spec(\mathbf{F})
(Example
3.7.3
),
\pr^{*}\mathcal{K}
is USLA over
\mathbf{G}_{m}
where
\pr:Y\times\mathbf{G}_{m}\to Y
is the projection (Lemma
3.7.2
).
Hence we may apply Proposition
7.3.1
.
∎

7.5.
Motivic local terms

We will now prove the following result, which appears in the case of
\ell
-adic sheaves on schemes in
[
Var07
, Theorem 2.1.3]
and in the case of motivic sheaves on schemes in
[
Jin24
, Theorem 5.2.14]
.

Given a correspondence
(Y\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Y)
of Artin stacks locally of finite type over a field
\mathbf{F}
and a closed substack
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow Y
stabilized by
c
, we consider again the base-changed correspondence
(Z\xleftarrow{c_{0Z}}C_{Z}\xrightarrow{c_{1Z}}Z)
as in (
7.2.1
).
Since the right square in (
7.2.1
) is Cartesian,
\iota
induces a right topologically pullable map of correspondences in the sense of Remark
4.3.3
, and there is a pullback operation
\iota^{*}
on cohomological correspondences (see §
4.3.2
).

Theorem 7.5.1
.

Let
c=(c_{0},c_{1})\colon C\rightarrow Y\times Y
be a correspondence of locally finite type Artin stacks over
\mathbf{F}
. Let
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed substack such that
C
is contracting near
Z
. Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})
. Then
\Fix(C_{Z})\rightarrow\Fix(C)
is open-closed, and for any
\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
, we have

\Tr_{C}(\cc)|_{\Fix(C_{Z})}=\Tr_{C_{Z}}(\iota^{*}\cc)\in\CH_{d}(\Fix(C_{Z})).

Lemma 7.5.2
.

The correspondence
c
is contracting near
Z
if and only if it stabilizes
Z
and the set-theoretic image of
\widetilde{c}_{0s}=N(c_{0})
is contained in the zero-section
Z\hookrightarrow N_{Z}(Y)
.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
7.2.4
.
See also
[
Jin24
, Lemma 6.4.2(1)]
.
∎

Lemma 7.5.3
.

Continuing to assume that
c
stabilizes
Z
, the commutative cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix(C_{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

has all squares Cartesian.

Proof.

Since
Z\hookrightarrow Y
is a closed embedding, we have
Z\cong Z\times_{Y}Z\cong(Z\times Z)\times_{Y\times Y}Y
, so the bottom square is Cartesian. The back face is Cartesian by definition. Hence the diagonal square with vertices
\Fix(C_{Z}),C_{Z},Y,Y\times Y
is Cartesian. The front face is Cartesian by definition, hence the top face is also Cartesian.

The right face is Cartesian by the assumption that
c
stabilizes
Z
. Hence the diagonal square with vertices
\Fix(C_{Z}),C,Z,Y\times Y
is Cartesian. As the front face is Cartesian, the left face is also Cartesian. We have now checked that all squares are Cartesian.
∎

Proposition 7.5.4
.

If
c
is contracting near
Z
, then the map
\Fix(C_{Z})\rightarrow\Fix(C)
is open-closed on reduced substacks.

Proof.

In the schematic case this is
[
Var07
, Theorem 2.1.3(a)]
, and the argument is the same in essence. By passing to an open subset of
C
, we may assume that
\Fix(C)
is connected and noetherian and
\Fix(C_{Z})
is non-empty.
Since
c
is contracting near
Z
, the map
\widetilde{c}_{0s}=N(c_{0})
has set-theoretic image contained in the zero-section
Z\hookrightarrow N_{Z}(Y)
by Lemma
7.5.2
. Hence the same holds for the composite map

N_{\Fix(C_{Z})}(\Fix(C))\to N_{C_{Z}}(C)\xrightarrow{N(c_{0})}N_{Z}(Y).

Under the identification
\Fix(C_{Z})\cong c^{\prime-1}(Z)
where
c^{\prime}:\Fix(C)\to Y
(Lemma
7.5.3
), it follows by Lemma
7.2.4
that
(c^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}^{n}\subset(\mathcal{I}_{{c^{\prime}}^{-1}(Z)})^{n+1}
for some
n
.
Since
(c^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}^{n}=(\mathcal{I}_{{c^{\prime}}^{-1}(Z)})^{n}
, we deduce that
(\mathcal{I}_{{c^{\prime}}^{-1}(Z)})^{n}=(\mathcal{I}_{{c^{\prime}}^{-1}(Z)})^{n+1}
for some
n
. The noetherianity and connectedness then imply that
(\mathcal{I}_{{c^{\prime}}^{-1}(Z)})^{n}=0
, so
\Fix(C)_{\red}\cong{c^{\prime}}^{-1}(Z)_{\red}\cong\Fix(C_{Z})_{\red}
.
∎

Proposition 7.5.5
.

If
c
is contracting near
Z
, then
\Fix(D_{C_{Z}}(C))_{\red}
is isomorphic to the constant family
\Fix(C_{Z})_{\red}
over
\mathbf{A}^{1}
.

Proof.

In the schematic case this is
[
Var07
, Theorem 2.1.3(b)]
, and the argument is the same in essence. By passing to an open subset of
C
, we may assume that
\Fix(C)
is connected and noetherian and
\Fix(C_{Z})
is non-empty. Then thanks to Proposition
7.5.4
we have
\Fix(C)_{\red}\cong\Fix(C_{Z})_{\red}
. As
\widetilde{(c_{Z})}_{\red}
is a constant family of correspondences over
\mathbf{A}^{1}
, we have
\Fix(C)_{\red}\times\mathbf{A}^{1}\cong\Fix(D_{C_{Z}}(C_{Z}))_{\red}
, which by Lemma
7.5.3
admits a closed embedding into
\Fix(D_{C_{Z}}(C))_{\red}
, which is moreover an isomorphism over
\eta\subset\mathbf{A}^{1}
. It then suffices to show that the special fiber
\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))
is set-theoretically supported within
\Fix(C)
.

By Lemma
7.5.2
,
\widetilde{c}_{0s}[\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))]
has set-theoretic image contained in the zero-section
Z\hookrightarrow N_{Z}(Y)
. Hence the same is true for
\widetilde{c}_{1s}[\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))]
, so
\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))
has set-theoretic image contained in the zero-section
C_{Z}\hookrightarrow N_{C_{Z}}(C)
under both
\widetilde{c}_{0s}
and
\widetilde{c}_{1s}
. But the restriction of
\widetilde{c}_{s}
to
c_{1}^{-1}(Z)
equals the correspondence
c_{Z}
, so we deduce that
\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))
is set-theoretically contained in
\Fix(C_{Z})\subset\Fix(C)
, as desired.
∎

Recall the specialization to the normal cone map
\mathrm{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}
on cohomological correspondences defined in §
7.4.3
.

Proposition 7.5.6
.

Suppose
c
is contracting near
Z
.
Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})
and let
\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
be a cohomological correspondence.
If
\mathrm{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}(\cc)=0
, then we have

\Tr_{C}(\cc)|_{\Fix(C_{Z})}=0\in\CH_{i}(\Fix(C_{Z})).

Proof.

By Corollary
7.4.3
we have

\mathfrak{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}(\Tr_{C}(\cc))=\Tr_{N_{C_{Z}}(C)}(\mathrm{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}(\cc))=0.

Since the map
\mathfrak{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}
is an isomorphism in this case by Proposition
7.5.5
, we conclude that
\Tr_{C}(\cc)=0
.
∎

Corollary 7.5.7
.

Suppose
c
is contracting near
Z
.
Let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot},\mathrm{gm}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})
and let
\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
be a cohomological correspondence.
If
\iota^{*}\mathcal{K}\cong 0\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Z};\mathbf{Q})
, then we have

\Tr_{C}(\cc)|_{C_{Z}}=0\in\CH_{i}(\Fix(C_{Z})).

Proof.

By Proposition
7.4.2
we have
\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}(\mathcal{K})|_{Z}\cong\mathcal{K}|_{Z}\cong 0
.
By Lemma
7.5.2
, the map
\widetilde{c}_{0s}
has set-theoretic image contained in the zero-section
Z\subset N_{Z}(Y)
, hence also
\widetilde{c}_{0s}^{*}\mathrm{sp}_{Y,Z}(\mathcal{K})\cong 0
.
In particular,
\mathrm{sp}_{C,C_{Z}}(\cc)=0
.
We conclude by Proposition
7.5.6
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.5.1
.

Since the diagram (
7.2.1
) is left pushable (as the vertical maps are closed embeddings, hence proper, cf. Example
4.2.3
),
\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\mathcal{K}_{0},\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
is defined.
By Proposition
6.2.1
we have
\Tr_{C}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}\cc)=\Fix(\iota)_{*}(\Tr_{C_{Z}}(\iota^{*}\cc))
.

Let
j\colon V\hookrightarrow Y
be the open embedding complementary to
\iota
. Since
Z
is
c
-invariant, we have
c_{0}^{-1}(V)\subset c_{1}^{-1}(V)
. Let
C_{V}\coloneqq c_{0}^{-1}(V)
. Then the map of correspondences

(7.5.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}

is right pullable (since the vertical maps are open embeddings, hence smooth, cf. Example
4.2.3
), so
j^{*}\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{V}}(j^{*}\mathcal{K},j^{*}\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
is defined. Since the left square of (
7.5.1
) is Cartesian, it is also left pushable, so
j_{!}j^{*}\cc\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C}(j_{!}j^{*}\mathcal{K},j_{!}j^{*}\mathcal{K}{\scriptstyle\langle-i\rangle})
is defined.
Since
\iota^{*}j_{!}j^{*}\mathcal{K}\cong 0
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Z};\mathbf{Q})
, we have
\Tr_{C}(j_{!}j^{*}\cc)|_{\Fix(C_{Z})}=0
by Corollary
7.5.7
.

We have

(7.5.2)

\Tr_{C}(\cc)=\Tr_{C}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}\cc)+\Tr_{C}(j_{!}j^{*}\cc)=\Fix(\iota)_{*}(\Tr_{C_{Z}}(\iota^{*}\cc))

by additivity of the trace (see
[
Jin24
, Lemma 5.2.7]
, whose proof works verbatim for stacks). Then restricting to
\Fix(C_{Z})
, and we conclude using that
\Tr_{C}(j_{!}j^{*}\cc)|_{\Fix(C_{Z})}=0
as found in the preceding paragraph.
∎

8.
Derived homogeneous Fourier transform

Let
E
be a vector bundle over a scheme
S
and
\widehat{E}
the dual vector bundle
17
17

17

We also use the notation
E^{*}
for the dual vector bundle to
E
. However, this leads to an inconvenient notation for the dual of the map, so we avoid it when discussing the Fourier transform. We note that the notation
E^{\vee}
is also reserved for the
Serre dual
of
E
.
. Laumon
[
Lau03
]
introduced a geometric Fourier transform

\mathcal{D}^{{\mathbf{G}_{m}}}(E;\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})\to\mathcal{D}^{{\mathbf{G}_{m}}}(\widehat{E};\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})

on bounded constructible derived categories of
homogeneous
(i.e.,
{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
-equivariant)
\ell
-adic sheaves.
It can be regarded as a uniform version of the
\ell
-adic Fourier transform of Deligne-Laumon
[
Lau87
]
(for base fields of characteristic
p>0
) and the
D
-module
Fourier transform of Brylinski–Malgrange–Verdier (for base fields of characteristic
0
).

We will describe an extension of this construction from vector bundles to
derived vector bundles
, i.e., total spaces of perfect complexes (cf.
[
FYZ22
, §6.1]
). As explained in §
2.4
, the total space of a perfect complex
\mathcal{E}
exists naturally as a derived Artin stack
\Tot(\mathcal{E})
, which can exhibit both derived and stacky behavior depending on the amplitude of the complex.
Thus in this context, the Fourier transform manifests a duality between derived and stacky phenomena.

For this section (only), the scope of the sheaf theory will be expanded significantly. In fact, we will show that the homogeneous Fourier transform is well-behaved in the context of any reasonable six functor formalism. More precisely, we will work in the generality of any
topological weave
in the sense of
[
Kha
]
. This includes classical six functor formalisms such as the derived
\infty
-category of sheaves of abelian groups (over
\mathbf{C}
) or the derived
\infty
-category of
\ell
-adic sheaves (over a base on which
\ell
is invertible), but also various motivic
\infty
-categories: Voevodsky motives, MGL-modules, or motivic spectra (not even orientability is required). We include this added generality because the arguments are uniform, and also for the sake of forthcoming applications in different sheaf-theoretic contexts.

Here is an outline of this section. We begin in §
8.2
by defining the homogeneous Fourier transform for derived vector bundles and stating our results about it. §
8.3
recalls the Contraction Principle and its consequences. §
8.4
carries out some technical computations. In §
8.4
we redo some straightforward computations from
[
Lau03
]
that don’t involve derived vector bundles, adapting the arguments from
op. cit.
to the generality we work in here. In §
8.5
we prove the easier properties that are independent of involutivity. The proof of involutivity is achieved in §
8.6
– §
8.8
. Finally the remaining properties of the Fourier transform are derived from involutivity in §
8.9
.

8.1.
Conventions and notation

8.1.1.
Sheaves

Throughout the section we fix a
topological weave

\mathbf{D}
, which is an axiomatization of a sheaf theory with the six functor formalism introduced in
[
Kha
]
.
Roughly speaking this is a six-functor formalism with the following properties:

(a)

Localization:

The
\infty
-category
\mathbf{D}(\varnothing)
is zero.
For any derived Artin stack
Y
and any closed-open decomposition
i:Z\to Y
,
j:Y\smallsetminus Z\to Y
, there is a canonical exact triangle of functors

(8.1.1)

j_{!}j^{*}\to\mathrm{id}\to i_{*}i^{*}.

(b)

Homotopy invariance:

For any derived Artin stack
Y
and vector bundle
\pi:E\to Y
, the unit morphism
\mathrm{id}\to\pi_{*}\pi^{*}
is invertible.

Note that localization implies
derived invariance
: for a derived Artin stack
Y
, the inclusion of the classical truncation
Y_{\mathrm{cl}}\hookrightarrow Y
induces an equivalence
\mathbf{D}(Y)\cong\mathbf{D}(Y_{\mathrm{cl}})
which commutes with each of the six functors.
We also have
Poincaré duality
, which for a topological weave gives a canonical isomorphism

(8.1.2)

f^{!}(-)\cong f^{*}(-)\langle{T_{f}}\rangle

for
f
any smooth morphism with relative tangent bundle
T_{f}
. (Here
\langle{T_{f}}\rangle
is the
Thom twist
by
T_{f}
.)
If
\mathbf{D}
admits an orientation (as in the first five examples below), we may identify
\langle{T_{f}}\rangle\cong\langle{d}\rangle\coloneqq(d)[2d]
where
d
is the relative dimension of
f
.

On (derived) schemes, examples of weaves are as follows:

(a)

Betti sheaves (over
\mathbf{C}
):

The assignment
Y\mapsto\mathbf{D}(Y)
sending
Y
to the derived
\infty
-category
\operatorname{D}(Y(\mathbf{C});\mathbf{Z})
of sheaves of abelian groups on the topological space
Y(\mathbf{C})
.

(b)

Étale sheaves (finite coefficients, over
k
with
n\in k^{\times}
):

The assignment
Y\mapsto\mathbf{D}(Y)
sending
Y
to the derived
\infty
-category
\operatorname{D}_{\et}(Y;\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z})
of sheaves of
\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
-modules on the small étale site of
Y
.

(c)

Étale sheaves (
\ell
-adic coefficients, over
k
with
\ell\in k^{\times}
):

The assignment
Y\mapsto\mathbf{D}(Y)
sending
Y
to the
\ell
-adic derived
\infty
-category
\operatorname{D}_{\et}(X;\mathbf{Z}_{\ell})
of sheaves on the small étale site of
Y
, i.e., the limit of
\operatorname{D}_{\et}(Y;\mathbf{Z}/\ell^{n}\mathbf{Z})
over
n>0
.

(d)

Motives:

For any commutative ring
\Lambda
, the assignment
Y\mapsto\mathbf{D}(Y)
sending
Y
to the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}(Y;\Lambda)\coloneqq\operatorname{D_{H\Lambda}}(Y)
of modules over the
\Lambda
-linear motivic Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum
H\Lambda_{Y}
(defined as in
[
Spi18
]
).

(e)

Cobordism motives:

The assignment
Y\mapsto\mathbf{D}(Y)
sending
Y
to the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{MGL}}}(Y)
of modules over Voevodsky’s algebraic cobordism spectrum
\mathrm{MGL}_{Y}
.

(f)

Motivic spectra:

The assignment
X\mapsto\mathbf{D}(Y)
sending
Y
to the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{SH}(Y)
of motivic spectra over
Y
.

The categories
\mathbf{D}(Y)
are symmetric monoidal, and we denote by
\mathbf{1}_{Y}
(or just
\mathbf{1}
when context is clear) the monoidal units.

When the weave satisfies étale descent, it can be extended to (derived) Artin stacks following the method of
[
LZ17
]
(see also
[
Kha19b
, App. A]
).
This is the case for the first three examples, as well as for the weave of interest in the rest of this paper,
Y\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}({Y};\mathbf{Q})
.

In general,
\mathbf{D}
only satisfies Nisnevich descent.
In that case the six-functor formalism extends to
\mathrm{Nis}
-Artin stacks (see
[
Kha
, §4]
,
[
KR22
, §1]
).
For
\tau\in\{\mathrm{Nis},\et\}
we define
(\tau,n)
-Artin and
\tau
-Artin stacks as in
[
KR22
, 0.2.2]
:

(i)

A stack is
(\et,0)
-Artin, resp.
(\mathrm{Nis},0)
-Artin, if it is an algebraic space (resp. a decent algebraic space).
Here an algebraic space is
decent
if it is Zariski-locally quasi-separated, or equivalently Nisnevich-locally a scheme.

(ii)

For
n>0
,
X
is
(\tau,n)
-Artin
if it has
(\tau,n-1)
-representable diagonal and admits a smooth morphism
U\twoheadrightarrow X
with
\tau
-local sections from some scheme
U
.
A stack is
\tau
-Artin
if it is
(\tau,n)
-Artin for some
n
.

For
\tau=\et
, these are the usual notions of
n
-Artin stacks and Artin stacks, while e.g.
(\mathrm{Nis},1)
-Artin stacks are the same as quasi-separated
1
-Artin stacks with separated diagonal by
[
LMB00
, §6.7]
.

If our chosen weave
\mathbf{D}
does not satisfy étale descent, then we adopt the convention that
Artin
means “
\mathrm{Nis}
-Artin”.

8.1.2.
\mathbf{G}_{m}
-Equivariant sheaves

If
X
is a derived Artin stack with
{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
-action, we define the
\infty
-category of
{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
-equivariant sheaves on
X
as the
\infty
-category of sheaves on the quotient stack:

\mathbf{D}^{{\mathbf{G}_{m}}}(X)\coloneqq\mathbf{D}([X/{\mathbf{G}_{m}}]).

There is a forgetful functor

\mathbf{D}^{{\mathbf{G}_{m}}}(X)\to\mathbf{D}(X)

defined by
*
-pullback along the (smooth) quotient morphism
X\twoheadrightarrow[X/{\mathbf{G}_{m}}]
.

For any
{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
-equivariant morphism
f:X\to Y
the four operations
f^{*}
,
f_{*}
,
f_{!}
,
f^{!}
are defined on the
{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
-equivariant category using the induced morphism
[X/{\mathbf{G}_{m}}]\to[Y/{\mathbf{G}_{m}}]
.
Since each operation commutes with smooth
*
-inverse image, they each commute with forgetting equivariance.

Note that, when the weave
\mathbf{D}
does not satisfy étale descent, we are implicitly using the fact that the group scheme
{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
is special in the sense of Serre (see
[
Ser58
, §4.1]
), so that
X\twoheadrightarrow[X/{\mathbf{G}_{m}}]
admits Zariski-local sections and hence the quotients are
\mathrm{Nis}
-Artin whenever
X
is.

8.1.3.
Derived vector bundles

In addition to §
2.4
, we will use the following notations and properties of derived vector bundles.

(a)

We write
E[n]\coloneqq\Tot(\mathcal{E}[n])
for every integer
n
.

(b)

We say
E
is
of amplitude
\geqslant 0
(resp.
\leqslant 0
,
[a,b]
) if
\mathcal{E}
is of tor-amplitude
18
18

18

We remind that we are using the
cohomological
indexing here.

\geqslant 0
(resp.
\leqslant 0
,
[a,b]
). Note that since
\mathcal{E}
is perfect,
E
is of some finite amplitude.

Notation 8.1.1
.

Given a derived vector bundle
E
over a derived Artin stack
S
, we denote by
\pi_{E}:E\to S
the projection and
0_{E}:S\to E
the zero section.

Notation 8.1.2
.

For every
E\in\operatorname{DVect}(S)
, it will be convenient to denote the quotient by the
\mathbf{G}_{m}
-scaling action by:

(8.1.3)

{}^{\dagger}{E}\coloneqq[E/\mathbf{G}_{m}].

Given a morphism of derived vector bundles
\phi:E^{\prime}\to E
, we also write
{}^{\dagger}{\phi}:{}^{\dagger}{E^{\prime}}\to{}^{\dagger}{E}
for the induced morphism.
We will also use the same notation for
\mathbf{G}_{m}
-invariant subsets of
E
, e.g.,
{}^{\dagger}{\mathbf{G}_{m,S}}=S
.

If the weave
\mathbf{D}
does not satisfy étale descent, we need the following:

Proposition 8.1.3
.

Let
S
be a derived stack and
E\in\operatorname{DVect}(S)
a derived vector bundle over
S
.
If
E
is of amplitude
\geqslant 0
, then the projection
\pi_{E}\colon E\to S
is affine.
If
E
is of amplitude
\geqslant-n
, where
n>0
, then
\pi_{E}
is
(n,\mathrm{Nis})
-Artin.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of
n
-Artinness which is well-known.
We recall it anyway for the reader’s convenience.
Suppose
E
is of amplitude
\geqslant-n
where
n=0
(resp.
n>0
).
It is enough to show that if
S
is affine, then
E
is affine (resp.
(n,\mathrm{Nis})
-Artin).
In the
n=0
case we have
E=\Tot(\mathcal{E})\cong\Spec(\Sym(\mathcal{E}^{*}))
(§
2.4
).
Thus assume
n>0
.

Let us assume the statement known for
n-1
and argue by induction.
Since
E[-1]
is of amplitude
\geqslant-n+1
, it is
(\mathrm{Nis},n-1)
-Artin by inductive hypothesis.
This implies that
E
has
(\mathrm{Nis},n-1)
-representable diagonal.

We now construct a
\mathrm{Nis}
-atlas for
E
.
Since
S
is affine we may choose a presentation of
\mathcal{E}
as a cochain complex of vector bundles (as in §
2.2
) and let
\mathcal{E}^{\leqslant 0}
and
\mathcal{E}^{>0}
denote the brutal truncations so that we have an exact triangle
\mathcal{E}^{>0}\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}^{\leqslant 0}
.
Taking total spaces we have a derived Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\leqslant 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{>0}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

We claim that
0\colon S\to E^{>0}
is smooth and admits Nisnevich-local sections.
Since
E^{\leqslant 0}
is affine by the
n=0
case,
i\colon E^{\leqslant 0}\to E
will then be a
\mathrm{Nis}
-atlas for
E
.

Let
T
be an affine derived scheme over
S
and
f:T\to E^{>0}
an
S
-morphism.
Since
E^{>0}
is of amplitude
<0
and
T
is affine, we have
\pi_{0}\Maps_{S}(T,E^{>0})\cong\{0\}
by the definition of
\Tot(-)
in §
2.4
.
In other words,
f
factors through the zero section
0:S\to E^{>0}
.
The base change of
0\colon S\to E^{>0}
along
f
is therefore identified with the projection
E^{>0}[1]\fibprod_{S}T\to T
.
The latter clearly admits a section over
T
(e.g. the zero section), and is smooth because
E^{>0}[1]
is of amplitude
[-n+1,0]
.
The claim follows.
∎

Remark 8.1.4
.

In fact one can easily show:
E
is of amplitude
\geqslant 0
if and only if
\pi_{E}
is affine, if and only if
\pi_{E}
is representable, if and only if
0_{E}
is a closed immersion.
Similarly,
E
is of amplitude
\leqslant 0
if and only if
\pi_{E}
is smooth, if and only if
\pi_{\widehat{E}}
is affine.

8.2.
Definition and properties of the derived homogeneous Fourier transform

8.2.1.
Homogeneous Fourier kernel

The quotient stack
{}^{\dagger}{E}
classifies pairs
(L,\phi\colon L\rightarrow E)
. Consider the homogeneous evaluation morphism

(8.2.1)

\ev_{E}:{}^{\dagger}{\widehat{E}}\fibprod_{S}{}^{\dagger}{E}\to{}^{\dagger}{\mathbf{A}^{1}_{S}}

sending a pair

\big((L,\phi:L\to\widehat{E}),(L^{\prime},\psi:L^{\prime}\to E)\big)

to

\big(L\otimes L^{\prime},L\otimes L^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\phi\otimes\psi}\widehat{E}\otimes_{S}E\xrightarrow{\ev}\mathbf{A}^{1}_{S}\big).

We define

(8.2.2)

\mathscr{P}_{E}\coloneqq\ev_{E}^{*}({}^{\dagger}{j_{*}}(\mathbf{1}))\in\mathbf{D}({}^{\dagger}{\widehat{E}}\fibprod_{S}{}^{\dagger}{E})

where
j:\mathbf{G}_{m,S}\to\mathbf{A}^{1}_{S}
is the inclusion and
{}^{\dagger}{j}:S={}^{\dagger}{\mathbf{G}_{m,S}}\hookrightarrow{}^{\dagger}{\mathbf{A}^{1}_{S}}
is the induced map of quotient stacks.

8.2.2.
Homogeneous Fourier transform

Let
\pr_{1}
and
\pr_{2}
denote the respective projections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{\dagger}{\widehat{E}}\fibprod_{S}{}^{\dagger}{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{\dagger}{\widehat{E}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{\dagger}{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

The
homogeneous Fourier transform
on
E
is the functor

\FT_{E}:\mathbf{D}^{{\mathbf{G}_{m}}}(E)\to\mathbf{D}^{{\mathbf{G}_{m}}}(\widehat{E})

defined by

(8.2.3)

\mathcal{K}\mapsto\pr_{1!}(\pr_{2}^{*}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathscr{P}_{E})[-1].

At times it will be convenient to think of
\FT_{E}
equivalently as a functor
\mathbf{D}({}^{\dagger}{E})\rightarrow\mathbf{D}({}^{\dagger}{\widehat{E}})
.

8.2.3.
Geometricity

We define the full subcategory
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{gm}}(-)
as in §
3.3
:

(a)

For
S
a derived scheme,
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{gm}}(S)
is the thick subcategory of
\mathbf{D}(S)
generated by all Thom twists of all
f_{\sharp}(\mathbf{1}_{T})
as
f\colon T\rightarrow S
ranges over smooth schemes over
S
, where
f_{\sharp}
is the left adjoint of
f^{*}
.

(b)

For
S
a derived Artin stack,
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{gm}}
is the full subcategory of objects that become geometric on any (equivalently all) atlas from a derived scheme.

The arguments in the proof of
[
CD19
, Theorem 4.2.29]
show that:

Theorem 8.2.1
.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a topological weave on derived schemes locally of finite type over a quasi-excellent scheme.
If
\mathbf{D}
is
\mathbf{Q}
-linear and satisfies étale descent and topological invariance (e.g.
\mathbf{D}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{mot}}(-;\mathbf{Q})
), then the six operations restrict to
\mathbf{D}_{\mathrm{gm}}
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
8.2.1
, consider the lisse extension of
\mathbf{D}
to derived Artin stacks (locally of finite type over a quasi-excellent scheme).
It is then immediate that the six operations preserve geometricity,
with the possible exception of
the operations
f_{*}
and
f_{!}
for
f
a non-representable morphism, just as in Remark
3.3.2
.
We will see in Corollary
8.4.2
that
\FT
also preserves geometricity in this case.

8.2.4.
Zero bundle

By abuse of notation, we denote the zero bundle
\Tot_{S}(0)=S
by
0_{S}\in\operatorname{DVect}(S)
.

Proposition 8.2.2
.

Let
o:B\mathbf{G}_{m,S}\to B\mathbf{G}_{m,S}
be the involution
L\mapsto\widehat{L}
.
There are natural isomorphisms
\FT_{0_{S}}\cong o^{*}\cong o_{*}\cong o_{!}\cong o^{!}
.

The proof is in §
8.5.1
.

8.2.5.
Involutivity

In the most general case, our statement of involutivity is up to twisting with a canonical
\otimes
-invertible object.

Lemma 8.2.3
.

Let
E\in\operatorname{DVect}(S)
.
The object

(8.2.4)
